###### Subject:

<!-- ¶4 -->
**Operation and Maintenance of Wastewater Facilities and Solids Management Program Services RFP-OCWUT-16-26**

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### **Dear Tasha and Members of the Selection Committee:**

<!-- ¶6 -->
This procurement represents a critical opportunity for the Oklahoma City Water Utilities Trust (OCWUT) to elevate operations and maintenance (O&M) through a more proactive, transparent, and asset-focused approach. Over the past decade, Jacobs has worked closely with every division within the Utilities Department, building a deep understanding of your facilities, business processes, and the people who operate them.

<!-- ¶7 -->
Through our site visits and due diligence, we have identified the key challenges facing the system today—and we understand the future challenges OCWUT must also navigate. Over the term of this partnership, OCWUT will be managing a changing biosolids and disposal strategy, major treatment expansion and disinfection upgrades, increasing pressure to optimize power and chemical use, and the need to protect significant capital investments as new assets are brought online. Jacobs brings the accountability, structure, operational discipline, and integrated technical resources to address both priorities: delivering measurable improvements from day one while helping OCWUT plan, integrate, and sustain the improvements that will define the system’s long-term performance.

<!-- ¶8 -->
With more than 40 years of O&M experience in Oklahoma and ongoing engineering work across the state, we have established strong working relationships with the Oklahoma Department of Environmental Quality (ODEQ) and other regulatory agencies. As shown in the timeline on the following page, we have a long history of supporting OCWUT—including the Hefner Water Treatment Plant (WTP) expansion, North Canadian Wastewater Treatment Plant (WWTP) work, the SCADA System Master Plan, and Deer Creek improvements—meaning we understand not only your system, but also your expectations. This familiarity enables us to transition seamlessly and begin delivering results immediately.

<!-- ¶9 -->
OCWUT is ambitious and positioned for performance excellence. As the only company in the water and wastewater industry to receive the Malcolm Baldrige National Quality Award, we understand what it takes to build a culture of accountability, discipline, and continuous improvement. Through our partnership, we’ll help position OCWUT to achieve that level of recognition in a way that reflects your people, priorities, and long-term vision.

<!-- ¶10 -->
**We ar** e pleased to submit a value-driven proposal built around what matters most:

<!-- ¶11 -->
- **Addressing the highest priorities from day one.** Our due diligence provided a clear view of the operational gaps, and we are prepared to act immediately, by providing:

<!-- ¶12 -->
- **Odor and H₂S control.** The H₂S conditions at North Canadian and Deer Creek affect nearby residents, reduce asset life, and present a significant safety hazard to personnel. We’ll address these conditions through targeted chemical addition at key points within the WWTFs, improve air changes with secondary auxiliary equipment, and point-source odor identification and remediation. These measures will support safe, reliable O&M activities across the facilities. Blackline Safety H₂S devices will serve as the primary personnel safety tool, providing continuous air monitoring and man-down notifications.

<!-- ¶13 -->
OCWUT-16-26 | O&M OF WASTEWATER FACILITIES AND SOLIDS MANAGEMENT PROGRAM SERVICES  | **1**

## Page 5

<!-- ¶1 -->
- **Process performance and compliance.** Fecal compliance exceedances, inadequate chlorination/dechlorination balance, and untended SCADA alarms and inaccurate instrumentation all point to a process control program that needs structure and accountability. Our structured process control program will have defined operating targets, flow-paced chemical dosing, and restored instrumentation—giving operators the tools to prevent permit excursions.

<!-- ¶2 -->
- **Deferred maintenance and asset condition.** OCWUT's facilities’ deferred maintenance has compounded over time, and the new contract rightly holds the operator accountable for thorough documentation. Our staffing approach will shift performing the tank drain and clean program along with minor PMs to operators. This shift allows our maintenance team to focus on preventative, predictive and corrective maintenance throughout the facilities. Within the first 90 days, we’ll complete a comprehensive condition assessment, establish a defensible baseline, and transition maintenance from reactive to proactive using our Asset Replacement Modeling (ARM) approach.

<!-- ¶3 -->
- **Staffing stability and accountability.** Staff turnover and wage competitiveness have been well-documented challenges under the current operator. We'll prioritize retaining qualified incumbent staff, implement competitive compensation and career development pathways, and leverage regional support resources to ensure consistent, reliable operations.

<!-- ¶4 -->
- **Transparent communication and performance measures that drive results.** We’ll implement a robust communication framework supported by real-time dashboards and structured reporting aligned with OCWUT’s performance requirements. This approach provides full visibility into safety, compliance, maintenance, and cost ensuring OCWUT always remains informed and in control.

<!-- ¶1 -->
- **Future innovations that lower costs and improve performance.** Beyond the services defined in the base scope, we bring proven tools and capabilities to reduce costs and improve performance, including Intelligent O&M analytics for chemical and energy optimization, structured odor evaluation programs, and accelerated implementation of OCWUT’s NexGen EAM platform. These are practical, implemented solutions already delivering results for utilities of similar size and complexity. Full details are provided in Section 8 Innovative and/or Alternative Recommendations.

<!-- ¶2 -->
- **More than an operator: integrated support from a proven partner to large utilities.** We deliver O&M services for some of the most complex utility systems in the country. In Jackson, Mississippi, we stabilized and now operate three WWTPs and 99 pump stations following emergency federal oversight—demonstrating our ability to restore performance in highly challenging environments. Jacobs has provided full-service water and wastewater O&M since 1980, supporting some of the largest and most complex public systems in the U.S.—including Baltimore (180 MGD), Wilmington (134 MGD), Baton Rouge (300 MGD), and Jackson (145 MGD).. For OCWUT, this means seamless integration between operations and capital improvements—from startup and commissioning to long-term asset integration— without requiring the City to bridge that gap.

<!-- ¶3 -->
- **A culture of doing things right.** Ethics and accountability are foundational to how we operate. Our expectation of “perfect compliance and perfect reporting of non-compliance” drives behaviors that align with our long-term partnership mindset and commitment to transparency. As a result, we are the go-to O&M firm for **c** ommunities looking to make a change.

<!-- ¶4 -->
- **A team built for OCWUT’s success.** Our highly qualified team combines local leaders who are familiar with OCWUT’s facilities, an experienced regional management team, and a deep bench of technical experts. Leading the charge is Project Manager **David Pitocchelli** , who knows these facilities firsthand from his previous work as project manager for this program. Assistant Project Manager **Alex Rodriguez** combines Oklahoma roots with 13 years of multi-facility operations experience, including leadership of a 180-MGD facility for Seattle Public Utilities. Project Director **Steve Carpenter** provides independent oversight, drawing on his leadership of Oklahoma City’s Hefner WTP and his role in stabilizing complex systems such as Jackson, Mississippi. Strategic Advisor **Rick Warner, PE, WEF Fellow** brings 25 years of water and wastewater leadership and a trusted relationship with OCWUT leadership. Together, this team brings the experience, continuity, and accountability needed to deliver results.

<!-- ¶5 -->
We commend OCWUT for modernizing the contract to reinforce your operational priorities and hold the operator accountable for results. However, the contract by itself cannot fully achieve the performance transformation you envision. Two additional prerequisites are critical: pricing that responsibly supports the level of service expected, and a cultural evolution within the staff to embrace consistent, disciplined execution. Jacobs’ proposal is designed not only to comply with the updated agreement but to support these broader conditions that ultimately determine success.

<!-- ¶6 -->
We are committed to serving as a trusted long-term partner to OCWUT and are deeply invested in your success, not only to deliver outstanding WWTP operations but also to help strengthen the City’s future achievements through our ongoing partnership. Executive Sponsor **Kevin Dahl** will serve as the primary point of contact for this procurement. Jacobs acknowledges receipt of Addendum No. 1 issued on April 22, 2026, and confirms our ability and willingness to meet the tentative deadline and provide any additional documentation required to award the Service Agreement as specified in this RFP. This confirmation ensures alignment with project timelines and facilitates a smooth selection process. Upon award, we’ll provide the required performance bond or irrevocable letter of credit, insurance documentation, and any other materials required under the RFP and Service Agreement.

###### **WE KNOW OKLAHOMA CITY—AND WHAT’S AT STAKE**

<!-- ¶5 -->
Combined Wastewater<br>Capacity Managed<br>40 YEARS<br>O&M Group Experience<br><!-- End of picture text -->

<!-- ¶6 -->
Our proposal isn’t built on assumptions. Before writing a single page, our team completed multi-day site visits at all five facilities, reviewed available data, and held focused strategy sessions with process, odor, maintenance, lab, automation, and safety subject matter experts (SMEs).

<!-- ¶7 -->
**What we found confirmed the urgency.** North Canadian’s headworks are inaccessible for routine maintenance due to H₂S readings up to 200 ppm. Only 3 of 30 odor scrubbers system-wide are operational. Both North Canadian and Deer Creek failed WET tests in Q4 2025. The current maintenance program is reactive—critical equipment chronically out of service, no documented repair plan, and cascading failure patterns repeating nearly every month.

<!-- ¶8 -->

###### **TECHNICAL APPROACH: FACILITY-SPECIFIC, RESULTS-DRIVEN**

<!-- ¶8 -->
Every element of our Technical Approach is grounded in what we observed during site visits and what the RFP requires. From day one, every team member will have clear line of sight to OCWUT's performance goals and how their role delivers them—making accountability part of how we work, not an afterthought. Here's how we'll deliver results across the areas that matter most:

<!-- ¶9 -->
**Compliance & Process Control:**  Real-time compliance trend monitoring with structured escalation protocols SRT-based wasting, DO optimization, and aeration right-sizing at every plant  Equipment upgrades at satellite labs plus operator training and online analyzer restoration to support data-driven process decisions **Odor & H₂S Control:**  A four-layer strategy—upstream source control at Witcher, equipment restoration, in-plant process adjustments, and targeted monitoring  Backed by proven results at Waterbury, CT (55%+ complaint reduction from source identification and odor control upgrades)  Understanding of priority repairs and CIP recommendations needed for odor control success

<!-- ¶10 -->
**Maintenance & Asset Management:**  Highly experienced Maintenance Manager and Asset Manager and on-site team supported by regional resources Robust asset management program and strong focus on preventive management execution  Predictive maintenance (vibration analysis, thermography, oil analysis) deployed from day one  NexGen EAM advantage—our consulting team is already OCWUT’s implementation consultant and brings deep NexGen expertise from other successful projects

<!-- ¶11 -->
**CIP Construction Integration:**  Dedicated CIP Construction Manager backed by Jacobs corporate engineering  Experience providing O&M input at 35%, 60%, and 100% design phases  Maintenance of Plant Operations (MOPO) planning that starts early and maintains full compliance throughout construction—no treatment disruptions

<!-- ¶12 -->
**Performance Transparency:** Real-time dashboards connected to data systems, giving OCWUT on-demand visibility into compliance status, odor data, and maintenance KPIs  Independent QA/QC oversight above day-to-day operations  Monthly Payment Requests that accurately self-report against all Schedule 5 requirements

<!-- ¶13 -->
BENEFIT TO OCWUT: _Your O&M team draws on process, maintenance, odor, SCADA, asset, and other experts across Jacobs — so root causes get solved once and optimization happens seamlessly. Every strategy ties to a measurable RFP outcome, and we inspire staff through a high-performance culture that explains the “why and how” each role affects the whole._

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **REQUIRED PLANS: BUILT FOR OCWUT, READY ON DAY ONE**

<!-- ¶2 -->
**Draft Transition Plan:**  Transition Manager Kelly Irving to lead a phased mobilization—from governance and due diligence through full operational control—with risk-based management addressing the 10 highest-probability risks Positions offered to all qualified incumbent staff A jointly signed baseline condition assessment completed before the Commencement Date

<!-- ¶3 -->
**Staffing & Training Plan (Schedule 10):**  Jacobs workforce of 96 FTEs (plus another 13 biosolids staff from our subcontractor) with 24/7 coverage at every facility  A high-performance culture built on competitive compensation, BeyondZero® safety, certification incentives for operators and mechanics, and cross-training across facilities—shifting the team from reactive response to proactive ownership of plant performance  Jacobs’ dedicated Resource Planning Group to fill vacancies fast and prevent turnover that has plagued current O&M

<!-- ¶4 -->
**Sludge Management Plan (Schedule 14):**  An integrated, generation-throughdisposal program covering all four facilities and ~170 permitted land application sites  Facility-specific strategies for North Canadian’s H₂S and plastics issues, Deer Creek’s hydrogen peroxide dosing protocol, Chisholm Creek’s liquid transfer coordination, and South Canadian’s construction-period continuity  All tied to a tiered Storage Management Protocol that keeps pad utilization well below Schedule 21 thresholds

<!-- ¶5 -->
**Solids Management Plan (Schedule 21):**  Strategic framework for dewatering, storage, hauling, and disposal as one coordinated system with clear operational triggers and escalation protocols aligned with our Odor Control and Process Control Plans  Solids, process performance, and odor managed together—and positioned to guide OCWUT through the transition from land application to landfill disposal and the Deer Creek heat drying integration

<!-- ¶6 -->
**Operational Integration Plan (Schedules 16 & 18):**  Structured, repeatable process for bringing each major CIP project—onsite hypochlorite generation at North Canadian and Deer Creek, the Deer Creek heat drying facility, and the systemwide biosolids disposal transition—from construction into daily operations  Detailed staffing impacts, maintenance adjustments, training sequences, and risk mitigation for each project  Backed by direct PSI MicroClor® operating experience at similar projects

<!-- ¶7 -->
BENEFIT TO OCWUT: _Plans built from verified, facility-specific intelligence—not boilerplate. Delivered on schedule, coordinated with the OCWUT General Manager, and structured to drive accountability from day one._

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### **JACOBS UNDERSTANDS OCWUT'S FACILITIES AND ITS GOALS FOR THIS O&M CONTRACT**

<!-- ¶3 -->
OCWUT’s wastewater system is one of the largest contract-operated programs in the country—four treatment plants and the Witcher Pump Station with a combined design capacity exceeding 110 MGD and peak flows above 180 MGD, a biosolids land application program spanning roughly 170 permitted sites, Hefner Water Treatment Plant (WTP) residuals management, and a septage receiving program. These facilities serve a growing metropolitan area entering a period of significant change, with nearly $200M in planned capital improvements over the next decade, including plant expansions, new disinfection systems, and a transition in biosolids handling.

<!-- ¶4 -->
In preparing this proposal, the Jacobs team thoroughly reviewed the RFP, Exhibit A schedules, and the Sample Service Agreement; attended both mandatory Pre-Proposal Conferences; conducted multi-day site visits across all five facilities; completed background document review; and held focused strategy sessions with process, odor control, solids, maintenance, laboratory, automation, and safety specialists. That preparation gave us a detailed, facility-specific understanding of where things stand today and what it will take to deliver the level of service OCWUT is looking for. Many of the challenges we identify below are also the drivers behind OCWUT’s significant CIP investments, and our approach is built to operate effectively through the transition while contributing O&M insight to the capital program. **Exhibit 1-1** provides a concise summary of our understanding of OCWUT’s goals, the key challenges facing these facilities, and the strategies Jacobs will bring to deliver results. Our strategies reflect the depth of our integrated O&M and engineering expertise, and we present each in detail throughout the corresponding sections of our Technical Approach.

<!-- ¶5 -->

**Minimize odors and meet Odor NC:** Headworks access is currently limited by H₂S concentrations **Control Plan performance metrics:** (readings up to 200 ppm at belt press). <!-- ¶4 -->
###### **Four-layer odor control strategy:**

<!-- ¶5 -->
**1) Source control:** Evaluate dissolved sulfide levels arriving at each plant; chemical dosing at Witcher (ferrous chloride or peroxide in wet well) to reduce H₂S before it reaches NC. **2) Equipment restoration:** Assess and rehabilitate BioWay tower at NC (engage Bio Air or ECS); replace exhausted carbon media with properly sized biological or chemical scrubbers where H₂S exceeds 10 ppm.

<!-- ¶6 -->
**3) In-plant process control:** Maintain aerobic conditions in critical process areas, actively manage sludge age and solids inventory to prevent H₂S formation at the source, and minimize detention time in sludge handling across all facilities. At DC specifically, separate WAS/primary sludge storage and optimize peroxide dosing ahead of the belt press.  Eliminate masking agents in favor of targeted source treatment.

<!-- ¶7 -->

## Page 17

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **MANAGEMENT PLAN**

<!-- ¶2 -->
**An Integrated Management Approach Built for OCWUT’s Complexity** OCWUT’s wastewater system—spanning four treatment plants, the Witcher Pump Station, and a complex solids management program—requires more than routine contract operations. **It demands an integrated management approach built on transparency, accountability, and a partnership structure that keeps OCWUT leadership always informed and in control** .

<!-- ¶3 -->
Jacobs will bring that approach. **Drawing on extensive expertise in delivering program management refined across large-scale contracts nationwide, we’ll establish a governance structure, communication cadence, and performance monitoring framework that gives OCWUT clear visibility into every aspect of facility operations, maintenance, compliance, and cost management** from day one and every day thereafter. This isn’t a bolt-on management layer; it’s how we operate. Structured accountability, proactive reporting, and collaborative decision-making are built into our management DNA.

<!-- ¶4 -->
###### **Leadership That Knows Oklahoma City**

<!-- ¶5 -->
**Leading this effort will be Project Director Steve Carpenter—a trusted name in Oklahoma City’s water community.** Steve ran the Hefner WTP and brings deep relationships with OCWUT staff built over years of local service. He’ll provide management oversight, drive compliance and communication, and serve as the independent quality assurance/quality control (QA/QC) voice on the project—reporting above the day-to-day project management structure to give OCWUT confidence that oversight is genuine and unfiltered.

<!-- ¶6 -->
Daily operations will be led by **Project Manager David Pitocchelli, who will lead daily operations and serve as OCWUT’s primary point of contact, and Assistant Project Manager Alex Rodriguez, who brings firsthand knowledge of OCWUT’s operations from his previous role in Oklahoma.** Together with Steve and our corporate support team, we’ll maintain a “zippering” communication approach, illustrated in **Exhibit 1-2** , that connects the right Jacobs leaders to the right OCWUT counterparts at every level of the organization.

<!-- ¶7 -->
###### PROVEN LEADERS TO BUILD A HIGH-PERFORMANCE CULTURE AND ELEVATE OCWUT FACILITIES’ O&M

<!-- ¶8 -->
Steve’s career centers on turning around large, complex O&M contracts—rebuilding teams, re-empowering staff, and lifting performance. He led the Jackson, Mississippi, water system transformation and the cultural reset at West Basin, California. Before that, he ran OCWUT’s Hefner WTP, so he already knows your culture, your community, and how OCWUT operates. Steve will anchor transition, drive compliance oversight, and keep communications with OCWUT transparent—holding our team accountable to the outcomes that matter to you.

## Page 19

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **O&M MANAGEMENT PHILOSOPHY/CRITICAL SUCCESS FACTORS**

<!-- ¶2 -->
Our approach to delivering results for OCWUT starts with seven tenets that define how we operate, collaborate, and continuously improve. These critical success factors, shown in **Exhibit 1-3** , create a holistic framework that connects every element of facility management into an integrated system focused on OCWUT’s priorities.

<!-- ¶3 -->
###### **Performance Management Framework** _Understanding and Commitment to Performance Standards_

<!-- ¶4 -->
OCWUT’s contract is performance-based. The Fixed Monthly Fee is directly tied to meeting the Performance Requirements defined in Schedule 5, and Jacobs takes that accountability seriously. Up to 32.4% of the monthly fee is at risk across Management, Compliance, Maintenance, and Staffing categories—a structure that aligns our priorities directly with OCWUT’s expectations for reliable, compliant operations.

<!-- ¶5 -->
We’ve studied these performance metrics carefully. They reflect OCWUT’s goals:

<!-- ¶6 -->

<!-- ¶10 -->
###### _How We’ll Track, Measure, and Report Performance_

<!-- ¶11 -->
Jacobs will implement an **integrated performance monitoring system that connects real-time operational data to the specific metrics** OCWUT cares about most. Our approach combines three layers of oversight, illustrated in **Exhibit 1-4** . _Corrective Action and Escalation_

<!-- ¶12 -->
When performance deviates from standards, our response will be immediate, documented, and traceable. Our process follows a structured escalation:

<!-- ¶13 -->
1. **Detection:** Real-time dashboards and daily operations monitoring flag deviations automatically

<!-- ¶14 -->
2. **Root Cause Analysis** : Operations and maintenance staff investigate and document the cause within 24 hours

<!-- ¶15 -->
3. **Corrective Action:** Project Manager/Operations Supervisor implements the fix and documents it in the CMMS

<!-- ¶16 -->
4. **Verification** : QA/QC Manager verifies the corrective action closed the issue

<!-- ¶17 -->
5. **Reporting** : Exception report provided to OCWUT with root cause, action taken, and prevention steps

<!-- ¶18 -->
###### **Jacobs’ QA/QC Program**

<!-- ¶19 -->
###### _Management Oversight_

<!-- ¶20 -->
Quality doesn’t happen by accident—it’s built into every process, verified through independent review, and corrected promptly when gaps are found. Jacobs’ QA/ QC Program for OCWUT will be led by Project Director **Steve** , who will provide management oversight independent of day-to-day operations, and supported by the full-time QA/QC Manager **Josh Alleman** reporting to Project Manager **David** with a direct line to **Steve** .

<!-- ¶21 -->
This structure, illustrated in **Exhibit 1-5** , directly addresses OCWUT’s Schedule 3 requirement for a Management Oversight and QA/QC Plan that goes “beyond general policy statements” and “clearly identifies the processes, responsibilities, and tools” used to manage performance, verify compliance, and correct deficiencies. This structure will further support oversight of Jacobs’ internal audit program, discussed below, and ensure audit results are acted upon to deliver results.

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### _QA/QC Plan Development and Internal Audit Program_

<!-- ¶2 -->
Upon contract award, Jacobs will develop a comprehensive QA/QC Plan (draft due November 2, 2026; final due December 31, 2026, per Schedule 3). The plan will define how we monitor daily operations, maintenance activities, regulatory compliance, safety practices, and reporting obligations. It will specify internal review procedures prior to report submittal and methods for tracking every required deliverable.

<!-- ¶3 -->
Our QA/QC program is anchored by an Annual Quality Audit—a structured process we’ve developed and refined over our O&M group’s 40-year history. This audit is quantifiable, repeatable, and leads to actionable results. A dedicated QA/QC team investigates 70 specific topics with a focus on contractual requirements, including training, project documentation, succession planning, communication plans, inventory, CMMS maintenance, work order status, sampling plan status, process control plans, data management, and business and budget management. The audit follows a four-step process, shown in **Exhibit 1-6** .

<!-- ¶4 -->
**EXHIBIT 1-6.** _Jacobs’ Four-Step Annual Quality Audit Process_

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### **<mark>PREPARATION</mark>**

<!-- ¶6 -->
- **STEP** 1 • Audit team sends information request to Project Manager • Alerts go to Compliance Team and regional O&M technical support of upcoming audit

<!-- ¶7 -->
- • Project Manager provides preliminary info for review

<!-- ¶8 -->
- **STEP** **<mark>ONSITE ASSESSMENT</mark>** 2 • Field assessments, staff interviews, and review of project records • Schedule follow-up within three weeks to discuss results

<!-- ¶9 -->
###### **<mark>EVALUATION</mark>**

<!-- ¶10 -->
STEP<br>3<br><!-- End of picture text -->

<!-- ¶11 -->
- Review information provided for completeness and accuracy ����������������������������

<!-- ¶12 -->
New projects are audited 6 months after startup, and the frequency of follow-up audits is determined by the project score across O&M and management areas. Results are compared to historical performance and benchmarked against all Jacobs’ O&M projects nationally giving OCWUT the benefit of lessons learned across our entire portfolio.

<!-- ¶13 -->
###### _Compliance Verification Before Report Submittal_

<!-- ¶14 -->
Every report delivered to OCWUT, including monthly operating reports, NPDES Discharge Monitoring Reports , Payment Requests, Maintenance and Repair Fund expenditure reports, Rehabilitation and Replacement expenditure reports, will undergo internal review before submittal. QA/QC Manager Josh will verify data accuracy, completeness, and consistency against source records. This is especially critical for the Monthly Payment Request, which includes self-reported compliance with Performance Requirements. The RFP makes clear that failure to accurately report compliance will result in consequences up to and including breach. We take that standard seriously and will build verification checkpoints into every reporting workflow.

<!-- ¶15 -->

<!-- ¶8 -->
###### _Jacobs Annual Innovation Workshop Drives Continuous Improvements and Elevated O&M_

<!-- ¶9 -->
Our **Annual Innovation Workshops—provided at no cost to OCWUT** —bring leading-industry experts to your team each year to review the prior year’s achievements and co-develop new goals aligned with OCWUT’s evolving needs and long-term vision. OCWUT staff, stakeholders, our on-site O&M team, and our utility experts all take part.

<!-- ¶10 -->
This forward-looking session keeps OCWUT at the leading edge of treatment technologies, optimization strategies, and modern O&M practices. We’ll handpick industry leaders in process engineering, biosolids and dryer technologies/ operations, odor control, hydraulics, wet weather flow management, pump station operations, digital optimization, and energy management to match your current challenges and opportunities.

<!-- ¶11 -->
Ahead of the workshop, we’ll co-develop a full-day agenda with you that reflects your priorities and sharpens the focus on your priorities. Our specialists then prepare by learning about your facilities, analyzing performance data, and pinpointing improvement opportunities so they arrive with actionable ideas tailored to OCWUT. During the session, we listen first, then co-create plans for the coming year. The result is measurable gains in operations, asset lifecycle performance, and cost efficiency. **For example, a previous workshop at the Wilmington WWTP identified opportunities for more than $1M in energy savings.**

Jacobs will organize our management systems around the critical areas of service delivery shown in **Exhibit 1-9** , with each system supported by detailed plans developed per Schedule 3.

## Page 25

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **Community Engagement**

<!-- ¶2 -->
**We’re already an active part of the Oklahoma City community** , as illustrated in **Exhibit 1-10** . With the O&M project, we intend to grow that engagement. Our approach will benefit both OCWUT and the communities it serves by making facilities a source of pride, growing the next generation of water professionals, and demonstrating that reliable wastewater operations and good neighbor practices go hand in hand by:

<!-- ¶3 -->
- <sup>**Helping the community understand how wastewater operations protect quality of life.**We’ll host periodic</sup> open house events for residents, schools, and community groups at OCWUT facilities to show how treatment operations protect public health, nearby waterways, and quality of life. We’ll participate in community events with practical public education materials that explain how proactive operations reduce odors, minimize service disruptions, and support reliable service.

<!-- ¶4 -->
- <sup>**Supporting education and growing the next generation.**We’ll support classroom visits, STEM outreach, and</sup> educational partnerships with local schools and colleges to help students understand treatment processes, water stewardship, and water sector careers. By introducing young people to the science and technology behind clean water, we’ll help build interest in the skilled workforce that utilities like OCWUT need.

<!-- ¶5 -->
- <sup>**Growing economic opportunities through workforce development.**We’ll create internships, mentoring</sup> programs, and career opportunities in partnership with local and regional schools and colleges to build a pipeline for water sector careers. <!-- ¶1 -->
###### **OPERATIONS PLAN**

<!-- ¶2 -->
Jacobs will implement a structured Operations Plan that brings consistency and control to day-today operations. The plan **establishes clear, repeatable procedures for operations, maintenance, safety, compliance, and reporting** —areas where variability today drives reactive performance and inconsistent outcomes.

<!-- ¶3 -->
It defines roles, routines, and escalation paths, so issues are identified and addressed early, not after they impact compliance or reliability. A formal SOP library will govern all critical activities, including startup and shutdown, routine monitoring, abnormal conditions, and work practices, so work is performed consistently across shifts, regardless of operator. SOPs will remain living documents, updated as equipment, permits, and operating conditions evolve, and reinforced through shift turnover, training, and routine supervisory oversight.

<!-- ¶4 -->
###### **PROCESS CONTROL STRATEGY**

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### **Jacobs Understands the Process Control Challenges and Goals for OCWUT’s Four Facilities: Process Stability Starts with the Fundamentals**

<!-- ¶6 -->
OCWUT’s four wastewater treatment facilities share a common challenge, process control fundamentals have eroded, and the resulting instability shows up in permit excursions, reactive operations, and equipment running well outside its intended operating envelope. Our observations during site visits confirmed what the compliance record shows: these facilities aren’t limited by their design. They’re operationally constrained by inconsistent solids management, underutilized instrumentation, and aeration systems running without meaningful feedback or control.

<!-- ¶7 -->
•<sup>**At North Canadian**, the plant had recently experienced an upset requiring re-seeding of the biological</sup> process. Air distribution across the aeration basins was poor—low dissolved oxygen (DO) at the front of the train and excessive DO at the tail end—suggesting the facility may not always need all four basins in service. Blowers have no variable frequency drives (VFDs) and rely on manual valves for air distribution, with DO probes positioned only at the unaerated influent and end of the aeration basins. Return activated sludge/ waste activated sludge (RAS/WAS) control appeared to follow a set-and-forget approach rather than active process management. The Phase 3 plant, effectively just a flow equalization basin (FEB), discharges directly to the combined effluent—a compliance risk any time untreated or septic flow reaches it. The Phase II chlorine contact basin had floating sludge with a permanently placed sludge judge, a sign of chronic solids carryover. These conditions, combined with the sulfur dioxide system’s inability to match chlorine demand during high flows, have driven the facility’s history of fecal coliform and chlorine residual violations. •<sup>**At Deer Creek**, mixed liquor suspended solids (MLSS) appeared very light with excessive DO and early-</sup> stage foaming—indicators of a young sludge age and over-aeration. RAS pumps had VFDs but were operating in static mode, not flow-paced. Secondary clarifiers showed excessive algae growth with no covered weirs, creating conditions where sloughing algae can blind the sand filters downstream. Sand filters were being manually backwashed rather than operated on automated control. Organic loading is approaching the facility’s design capacity, blower reliability has been a persistent problem (typically only two of four can run simultaneously

<!-- ¶8 -->
OCWUT-16-26 | O&M OF WASTEWATER FACILITIES AND SOLIDS MANAGEMENT PROGRAM SERVICES  | **21**

## Page 27

<!-- ¶1 -->
without faulting), and the anoxic selector zones have been out of service since 2019. The sludge holding tanks that receive both WAS and primary sludge have no mixing or aeration, creating the septic conditions that drive the extreme H₂S levels in the belt press room—levels high enough that our team could not enter the room during the site visit. •<sup>**At Chisholm Creek**, aeration basins were</sup> over-aerated with excessive DO and all blowers running energy spent without process benefit. Both bar screens at the headworks pump station were out of service. The backwash return tank has an overflow piped directly to the effluent, a compliance risk if sampled during overflow conditions. That said, Chisholm Creek has the fewest historical permit excursions and the most resilient overall performance among the four plants. •<sup>**South Canadian**will enter the contract term</sup> as a newly constructed 8.66-MGD activated sludge facility with on-site hypochlorite generation, disc thickeners, and new cloth filtration. The operational challenge here isn’t recovery—it’s getting the fundamentals right from day one, so the new plant establishes strong compliance margins and operating discipline before issues develop.

<!-- ¶2 -->
**The common thread across all four facilities is clear: biological process control, solids inventory management, aeration optimization, and instrumentation reliability are the levers that will move the needle on compliance and operational stability.** That’s where our process control strategy starts. **Exhibit 1-11 briefly summarizes process control priorities** we’ve identified based on site visits, facility data review, and compliance record analysis **.** Following the exhibit, we provide a detailed discussion of our full approach by facility.

<!-- ¶4 -->
Each facility’s Unit Process Control Plan (UPCP) will define specific target operating parameters and metrics aligned with Schedule 3 requirements— **solids retention time** (SRT) targets by season and loading condition, target MLSS concentrations for each treatment train, DO setpoints by basin zone, sludge blanket depth limits for secondary clarifiers, effluent quality targets for total suspended solids (TSS), biochemical oxygen demand (BOD), ammonia, and chlorine residual that provide operating margin below NPDES permit limits. During transition, we’ll establish initial targets based on design data, historical performance, and conditions observed during our site assessments, then refine them as we collect operating data and confirm biological process response. These parameters and metrics will be documented in the UPCPs, reviewed weekly during process control meetings, and formally updated at least annually or sooner when conditions, equipment, or permit requirements change.

###### _North Canadian WWTP — Stabilize First, Optimize Second_

<!-- ¶30 -->
measurable quantity of solids to waste each day. By maintaining a controlled solids inventory, operators will better protect secondary clarifier performance, reduce the likelihood of TSS and BOD excursions, and align sludge production with downstream solids handling and dewatering capacity, as illustrated in **Exhibit 1-13** .

<!-- ¶31 -->
At North Canadian, our process control strategy will focus first on re-establishing and sustaining permit compliance through stabilization of biological treatment, secondary clarification, and hydraulic performance. Past compliance challenges have been driven by hydraulic instability, equipment reliability issues, and limited operational flexibility, which together reduced the plant’s ability to manage solids inventory and respond effectively to wet weather events. The immediate objective is to stabilize the process, protect clarifiers, and restore predictable performance under both dry- and wet- weather conditions.

<!-- ¶32 -->
Secondary clarifier performance will be further strengthened through routine sludge blanket monitoring and trending, combined with ongoing solids flux and state point analysis. These tools will help operators understand how MLSS concentration and settling characteristics affect available clarifier capacity, particularly during wet weather. This information will guide decisions on wasting, flow routing, and step feed operation to prevent hydraulic overload and solids washout.

<!-- ¶33 -->
**A core element of our compliance recovery strategy will be implementing SRT-based wasting.** This approach links daily wasting volumes directly to a defined biological target rather than relying on fixed schedules or reactive adjustments. SRT-based wasting improves control of biological activity, reduces the risk of sudden biomass loss or over accumulation, and gives operators a specific,

<!-- ¶7 -->
the tail end. Increased DO profiling will allow operators to confirm that aeration is being applied where it’s needed, prevent localized low oxygen conditions that contribute to process instability or odor generation, and identify whether all basins need to remain in service at current loading levels. In the near term, enhanced monitoring will support day-to-day operational awareness. Over time, the data will establish a reliable aeration performance baseline, positioning the facility for future optimization efforts, including refined aeration strategies and potential DO-based control once process stability and instrumentation reliability are firmly established.

<!-- ¶8 -->
The over-aeration conditions observed across the system represent one of the most direct opportunities to reduce energy consumption. Aeration typically accounts for the largest share of electricity use at activated sludge facilities, and OCWUT’s baseline data confirms significant energy intensity—averaging approximately 1,964 kWh/MG at North Canadian/Witcher. Our DO monitoring and optimization efforts will tie directly into the required Energy Efficiency Plan. By right-sizing aeration to actual biological demand, we’ll work toward maintaining energy usage within the +5% variance of the established baseline while improving treatment performance.

<!-- ¶9 -->
As compliance stability is achieved, near term enhancements will focus on wet weather resilience and process flexibility. Step feed operation will be evaluated and implemented as appropriate to better distribute organic loading during peak flow events and protect clarifier performance. Existing ferric or ferrous systems will be assessed for chemically enhanced primary treatment during extreme wet weather to reduce solids and organic loading to the activated sludge process and provide additional protection against washout.

<!-- ¶10 -->
Longer term process control improvements will be pursued once consistent compliance is demonstrated. These include evaluating opportunities to add or reintroduce anoxic selector zones to improve settling and reduce aeration demand, assessing underutilized basins for solids reduction or aerobic digestion, and selectively rehabilitating filtration assets to add process stability during upset conditions. All improvements will be prioritized based on measurable benefits to compliance with reliability and operational sustainability.

<!-- ¶11 -->
###### _Deer Creek WWTP— Breaking the Cycle of Reactive Operations_

<!-- ¶12 -->
At Deer Creek, the process control strategy will focus on restoring and maintaining compliance through improved solids handling, filtration reliability, and disinfection stability. Previous permit excursions have largely been tied to inconsistent wasting practices, manual filtration operation, and unstable disinfection control during flow variability. The site visit confirmed these conditions: MLSS was light with excessive DO and early-stage foaming, RAS pumps were running in static mode rather than flow-paced, and the sand filters were being backwashed manually.

<!-- ¶13 -->
Initial actions will include implementing SRT-based wasting to stabilize biological activity and give operators predictable, day-to-day control of solids inventory. This will support stable secondary clarifier performance, more consistent sludge management, and improved coordination with downstream solids handling. Filtration systems will be returned to full automated control with routine turbidity and TSS monitoring to optimize backwash cycles and maintain consistent effluent quality. Disinfection reliability will be improved by tying chlorine and sulfur dioxide pacing directly to process flows and treatment conditions.

<!-- ¶14 -->
OCWUT-16-26 | O&M OF WASTEWATER FACILITIES AND SOLIDS MANAGEMENT PROGRAM SERVICES  | **24**

## Page 30

<!-- ¶1 -->
We recognize that long-term CIP improvements are planned to address the severe H₂S conditions in the belt press room. In the short term, our focus will be on operational adjustments that we can implement immediately. We’ll evaluate existing solids handling strategies for both WAS and primary sludge to reduce excessive detention times and uneven loading to the sludge holding tanks, which currently receive both streams with no mixing or aeration, creating the septic conditions that drive H₂S levels. By improving the timing, blending, and frequency of sludge transfers and pressing operations, we’ll limit septic conditions and reduce sulfide generation, providing rapid relief from localized odor impacts while longerterm solutions are evaluated.

<!-- ¶2 -->
Near term enhancements will include re-implementing the existing anoxic selector zones—offline since 2019 but with submersible mixers replaced and ready to return to service—to improve settling performance and reduce aeration demand. Expanded DO monitoring will address the over-aeration observed during our site visit (baseline energy intensity averages 1,763 kWh/MG at Deer Creek) while laying out the groundwork for future aeration control optimization. Longerterm efforts will focus on refining aeration efficiency, improving solids handling reliability, and using operating data to enhance performance while maintaining compliance margins.

<!-- ¶3 -->
###### _Chisholm Creek WWTP—Protecting What’s Working, Reducing What’s at Risk_

<!-- ¶4 -->
Chisholm Creek has experienced fewer historical permit excursions but faces ongoing operational risk from aging equipment, filtration reliability challenges, and clarifier limitations. The process control strategy here emphasizes proactive risk reduction to prevent future compliance issues rather than recovering existing ones.

<!-- ¶5 -->
Immediate efforts will focus on restoring dependable filtration automation and backwash control, supported by turbidity monitoring to protect effluent quality and downstream disinfection performance. The backwash returns tank overflow— currently piped directly to the effluent—will be closely managed to mitigate compliance risk during overflow conditions. Secondary clarifier operation will be strengthened through routine sludge blanket monitoring and trending.

<!-- ¶6 -->
SRT-based wasting will provide consistent control of biological inventory, improve hydraulic loading through the treatment process, and stabilize sludge management. This will also support production of thicker waste sludge, reducing the volume of water hauled off site to North Canadian and improving operational efficiency. The over-aerated conditions we observed—excessive DO with all blowers running (baseline energy intensity averages 2,292 kWh/MG)—will be addressed by rightsizing aeration to actual demand.

<!-- ¶7 -->
Over the near to long term, process control improvements at Chisholm Creek will focus on incremental enhancements that improve reliability without adding unnecessary complexity via practical, operator-driven, process control tools that are sustainable with existing staff and resources.

<!-- ¶8 -->
###### _South Canadian WWTP— New Plant, Right Habits from Day One_

<!-- ¶9 -->
South Canadian will enter service as a new 8.66-MGD activated sludge facility during the term of this contract. While the plant will benefit from modern design and new equipment, the primary objective will be to establish strong process control fundamentals from the outset and set a high bar for regulatory compliance and consistency.

<!-- ¶10 -->
Early operations will prioritize stabilizing the biological process through SRT-based wasting, providing operators with clearly defined daily wasting targets as influent characteristics and loading patterns are established. This disciplined approach will protect secondary clarifiers and support reliable wet weather performance during startup and early operation. Wet weather management will be emphasized through careful monitoring of hydraulic loading, flow distribution, and solids inventory, with proactive adjustments to preserve treatment performance and compliance margins.

<!-- ¶11 -->
Strong process monitoring and operator engagement will be integral to embedding good operating practices early in the facility’s lifecycle. Routine reviews of biological, hydraulic, and solids data will establish clear operating targets and standard responses, supporting consistent decision-making and operational confidence.

<!-- ¶12 -->
South Canadian will include **on-site sodium hypochlorite generation (OSHG)** using PSI MicroClor units, **a system our team has direct operating experience with at our Pampa, Texas, facility** . We’ll leverage that experience to optimize performance from the outset, including maintaining consistent hypochlorite strength, maximizing system reliability, managing the proprietary cartridge replacement cycle, and integrating hypochlorite production with plant disinfection demand. Preventive maintenance, performance monitoring, and best practice SOPs will maintain safe operation and reliable disinfection capacity.

<!-- ¶13 -->
As operational data becomes available, process control strategies will be refined to optimize SRT targets, wet weather response, and disinfection efficiency. By embedding disciplined process control practices early and leveraging proven operating experience, we’ll position South Canadian for reliable compliance, operational consistency, and long-term sustainability.

###### **How We Monitor, Verify, and Elevate Performance**

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### _Operating Under Low-Flow and Minimum-Load Conditions_

<!-- ¶3 -->
Our process control strategy also addresses low-flow and minimum-load conditions—periods that carry their own operational risks. During sustained dry weather, reduced hydraulic loading can lead to excessive solids retention times, over-aeration relative to actual oxygen demand, and elevated detention times that promote septic conditions in underutilized basins.

<!-- ¶4 -->
Jacobs’ process control strategy serves as the focal point for daily operations by maintaining steady-state treatment, preventing process upsets, and supporting permit compliance through effective monitoring, verification, and reporting. **We align process control with sampling, laboratory testing, field observations, and routine performance review, so operators identify changing conditions early and respond in a timely, consistent way.** UPCPs define targets, control limits, monitoring points, and response actions by unit process, grounded in plant design, influent characteristics, performance goals, and applicable permit requirements.

<!-- ¶5 -->
- <sup>**At North Canadian**, where the three-plant configuration provides significant</sup> operational flexibility, we'll take basins out of service during low-flow periods to maintain appropriate velocities and loading rates through active treatment trains, reducing energy consumption while preventing stagnation.

<!-- ¶6 -->
Our process control strategy operates in tandem with the Sampling and Testing QA/QC Plan (Schedule 3), which provides the data foundation for every process decision we make. Operators can’t control what they can’t measure, and compliance starts with data you can defend.

<!-- ¶7 -->
- <sup>**At Deer Creek**, where organic loading already approaches design capacity during</sup> normal conditions, low-flow periods present an opportunity to recover biological stability, rebuild solids inventory, and perform targeted maintenance on blowers, filters, and clarifiers.

<!-- ¶8 -->
Process control is carried out through a disciplined weekly rhythm supported by the proven tools summarized in **Exhibit 1-14** . We document weekly process control decisions, formally review and refresh the process control plan at least annually, and **equip both on-site staff and OCWUT with tools that streamline reporting and provide immediate access to system information** , as illustrated in **Exhibit 1-15** .

<!-- ¶9 -->
- <sup>**At Chisholm Creek and South Canadian**, low-flow strategies will focus on</sup> adjusting SRT targets, reducing basin service, and throttling back aeration to match actual demand—preventing the over-aerated conditions we observed at both facilities during our site visits.

<!-- ¶7 -->
###### **Process Control Doesn’t Operate in a Silo**

<!-- ¶8 -->
**Our approach to process control recognizes the interdependencies with odor control, wet weather management, solids and residuals management, energy efficiency, and maintenance/reliability.** Those topics are addressed in detail in their specific subsections of this Technical Approach.

<!-- ¶9 -->
For example, process optimization and energy efficiency are linked at every facility. The aeration improvements described in our facility-by-facility strategies, including expanded DO monitoring, right-sized basin service, and future DO-based control, directly support the Energy Efficiency Plan and OCWUT’s expectation that electricity usage stay within +5% of the established baseline per facility per contract year.

<!-- ¶10 -->
Process control decisions also inform how we manage standby and redundant equipment. Our Equipment Rotation Plan (Schedule 3) will provide equal annual service time for all parallel equipment, including pumps, blowers, clarifier drives, and filtration systems, so standby units are ready when needed. For equipment temporarily out of service, we’ll follow Schedule 3’s specific requirements: cleaning and lubricating mixing equipment, rotating all rotating equipment weekly, keeping electrical motor heaters energized to prevent condensation, and purging chlorine systems with nitrogen gas. This is particularly relevant at North Canadian, where the multi-plant configuration means some treatment trains may be idle during low-flow periods, and at Deer Creek, where blower reliability challenges make it critical that standby units stay in ready condition. Preventive maintenance will continue on all equipment whether in active service or not—a discipline that current operations have not consistently maintained.

###### **Laboratory and QA/QC Integration**

<!-- ¶11 -->
Reliable compliance depends on reliable data. Jacobs will build and operate a laboratory program at OCWUT that is accurate and fully integrated with operations.

<!-- ¶12 -->
Our due diligence confirmed that the current laboratory is operating without ODEQ facility-level certification, though Oklahoma regulations allow compliance reporting from a laboratory whose director holds the proper credentials. We will operate the laboratory in full compliance with all applicable permit requirements and approved analytical methods from day one. Leading Jacobs' laboratory program at OCWUT is Laboratory Manager **Scott Golden** , who brings 21 years of water and wastewater laboratory and operations experience, a Class A Oklahoma Wastewater Laboratory Operators License, and direct ODEQ compliance experience managing laboratory services, DMR preparation, and IPP coordination at an Oklahoma wastewater utility—meeting the Schedule 10 staffing requirement from day one. Scott will oversee all sampling programs, analytical testing, data validation, recordkeeping, and regulatory reporting across all four facilities. Supporting him is Laboratory O&M SME **Tommy Niknezhad** , who personally conducted the OCWUT laboratory due diligence during our March 2026 site visit and brings 10 years of expertise in compliance monitoring, sampling programs, and QA/QC implementation. Together, they bring the credentialed oversight, rigorous internal QA/QC protocols, and site-specific knowledge to build a defensible, reliable laboratory program that supports accurate, timely DMR submissions.

<!-- ¶13 -->
The Sampling and Testing QA/QC Plan, required by Schedule 3 with a draft due November 2, 2026, will govern all sampling and analysis activities, covering procedures for sampling and testing untreated and treated wastewater, operating the laboratory in conformance with all applicable laws, and tying laboratory data directly to process control plan targets, odor control plan requirements, and NPDES permit conditions. All analytical methods will follow EPA-approved standards, with chain-of-custody procedures, calibration verification, and internal data validation maintained before any data is submitted.

<!-- ¶14 -->
Beyond the North Canadian central laboratory, site visit observations identified significant limitations at satellite facilities. Deer Creek and Chisholm Creek can run field compliance parameters only, with no oven or balance for solids analysis. South Canadian is similarly limited. This creates a dependency on North Canadian for all process control data. Jacobs will account for this constraint in staffing and sampling schedules to avoid gaps in daily operational data.

<!-- ¶7 -->
###### **ODOR CONTROL STRATEGY**

<!-- ¶8 -->
###### **Why Odor Management and Control Matters to OCWUT**

<!-- ¶9 -->
Odor is one of the strongest indicators of system performance. It's what your customers experience first, and it directly shapes how your facilities are perceived by the community.

<!-- ¶10 -->
OCWUT's system inherently carries elevated odor risk. Long force mains create conditions for septicity and hydrogen sulfide formation before flow reaches the plant. Pump stations and wet wells act as localized hotspots. Facilities like Deer Creek operate near residential areas where even minor issues quickly become highly visible. Based on our site observations, these risks are driven by a combination of upstream conditions, process variability, equipment availability, and solids handling practices. Elevated hydrogen sulfide levels in dewatering buildings and belt press operations at North Canadian and Deer Creek have already restricted access for routine O&M, delaying essential work and increasing safety risk for staff.

<!-- ¶11 -->
When odor isn't controlled at the source, the consequences compound. Complaints increase, regulatory attention follows, and operations become reactive, relying on excessive chemical use to compensate for instability. Costs rise, reliability falls, and public confidence erodes. Odor is not just an environmental issue. It is a system performance issue, and we will manage it as one. We'll replace the current reactive approach with structured, data-driven control, managing odor through stable processes, reliable equipment, and clear operational accountability. **Given that odor has been a persistent challenge at these facilities, with multiple studies conducted over the years, we've developed a more comprehensive approach to getting ahead of it. Section 8 details the innovative tools and strategies we'll bring to bear, going beyond conventional chemical treatment to address odor at its source.**

<!-- ¶12 -->
###### **A Systemwide Approach to Odor Management**

<!-- ¶13 -->
**We’ll implement a structured, systemwide approach built around four actions: prevent, detect, respond, and improve.**

<!-- ¶14 -->
We’ll **prevent** odor by stabilizing process conditions and controlling hydrogen sulfide generation at the source, maintaining appropriate dissolved oxygen, managing solids retention time, and minimizing detention time in wet wells, force mains, and sludge storage.

We’ll **detect** odor through routine field presence and targeted monitoring. Operators will patrol both facilities and the collection system using portable hydrogen sulfide meters, with off-site patrols conducted during known highodor periods. All findings will be logged with time, weather conditions, and wind direction, creating a traceable record of field conditions.

<!-- ¶18 -->
We’ll **respond** quickly and consistently, with written guidelines in place so operator response is immediate and consistent rather than improvised ( **Exhibit 1-18** ). We’ll **improve** continuously by tracking odor data, complaint history, and operational metrics to refine chemical dosing, adjust process control, and target improvements where they're needed most. This approach replaces reactive responses with consistent, repeatable performance.

<!-- ¶19 -->
###### **Collection System Odor Control**

<!-- ¶20 -->
Odor control begins upstream. At OCWUT, our site observations confirmed that a significant portion of odor originates in the collection system before flow ever reaches the treatment facilities. Long force mains and pump stations, including major conveyance from the Witcher Pump Station, create extended detention times that allow septicity and hydrogen sulfide formation to develop well upstream of the plants. Controlling odor at its source is more effective and more efficient than managing it at the fence line ( **Exhibit 1-19** ).

<!-- ¶21 -->
Day-to-day operations structured around our “ **prevent, detect, respond, and improve” framework** will address these upstream drivers through optimized wet-well management, refined chemical dosing, and integrated field data. But operational control alone isn't enough to solve a system with OCWUT's complexity and history. We will bring a second, deeper capability: the engineering expertise to quantify odor and corrosion across the system, determine off-site impacts, evaluate control alternatives, and select the most cost-effective solutions before

<!-- ¶22 -->
OCWUT-16-26 | O&M OF WASTEWATER FACILITIES AND SOLIDS MANAGEMENT PROGRAM SERVICES  | **35**

model in the world proven on a significant number of projects to accurately predict odors, corrosion, and the performance of mitigation alternatives across complex collection systems. **We’ve applied WATS on more than 35 projects, collaborating with Aalborg University and participating in the $20M Australian Sewer Corrosion and Odor Research (SCORe) program to help refine and validate the model.** WATS simulates sulfide generation, odor release, and corrosion risk across the entire network, identifying hotspots not visible through field observation alone ( **Exhibit 1-20** ). It can also be linked with wastewater treatment process models to determine how upstream conditions affect plant performance, directly supporting our integrated approach at OCWUT. We will use WATS to evaluate chemical dosing strategies, optimize dosing locations and rates, and assess alternatives before implementation, eliminating the costly trial-and-error currently observed in operations and delivering meaningful cost savings on chemical spend.

Wet-weather events create a distinct and predictable odor risk that requires proactive management. When flows exceed treatment capacity and the wet-weather management basins at North Canadian and Witcher Pump Station are activated, settled sludge accumulates on basin floors as flow slows. As that material becomes septic, it drives significant hydrogen sulfide generation when basins are returned to service. We’ll manage this risk through proactive basin management before, during, and after wet-weather events. Post-event, we’ll promptly initiate drawdown

###### **Maintenance as a Driver of Odor Control**

<!-- ¶10 -->
Odor conditions are not only a process issue. They are directly tied to maintenance practices. Current conditions indicate a reactive maintenance approach, where critical equipment remains out of service for extended periods and repairs are deferred or bypassed. This “firefighting” model contributes directly to odor generation, particularly where grit systems, headworks equipment, and solids handling processes are not consistently available. When these systems are

<!-- ¶11 -->

At contract start, our odor control experts will collect samples from odorous treatment processes and analyze them for hydrogen sulfide and reduced sulfur compounds. Combined with site-specific atmospheric data, dispersion modeling will identify the most likely sources of off-site odor impact at each facility, prioritize improvements for the greatest community

###### **Advanced Monitoring and Data-Driven Control**

<!-- ¶5 -->
We will implement a targeted monitoring strategy at high-risk locations across all four facilities, including key process areas, pump stations, force main discharge points, and representative fence-line locations. This data will establish action thresholds that trigger operational responses, replacing the inconsistent manual process control observed during our site visits with clear operating targets, realtime visibility, and defined response actions ( **Exhibit 1-22** ).

<!-- ¶6 -->
**As part of our value-added investment in OCWUT's odor program, we will install Sulfilogger sulfide monitors at each facility and weather stations to monitor wind speed and direction. This $30K investment delivers two immediate operational benefits.** First, Sulfilogger monitors will allow ferric chloride dosing to be automated and controlled proportionally to actual sulfide concentrations in real time. Currently, plant staff wait for odors to occur before activating ferric at a constant rate, which results in either overdosing or insufficient feed during concentration peaks. Sulfilogger-driven automation eliminates that reactive cycle, improving odor control while reducing ferric consumption and cost. Second, weather station data, combined with odor monitors mounted near the plant perimeter, will feed directly into an air dispersion model that generates a real-time graphic understanding of plume dispersion and travel into adjacent neighborhoods. This capability moves odor management from reactive complaint response to predictive community protection, giving operators advance warning when conditions are likely to push odors toward residential areas and time to act before complaints occur.

<!-- ¶7 -->
Together, these tools provide focused identification of significant odor sources and dispersion patterns, with prioritized improvement recommendations that minimize

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### **Biosolids and Disinfection Systems as Odor Drivers**

<!-- ¶3 -->
OCWUT operates a large and complex biosolids land application program, with approximately 170 permitted sites and more than 13,000 acres. Site observations identified several challenges that directly affect odor, including high moisture content in cake, debris in biosolids, storage limitations, and hydrogen sulfide generation in dewatering areas. OCWUT's requirements explicitly link biosolids management to odor control performance, making consistent solids quality and handling practices critical to systemwide odor control.

<!-- ¶4 -->
We will manage biosolids with a focus on consistency, stability, and odor reduction. This includes improving dewatering performance, reducing storage time, coordinating hauling and land application activities, and evaluating alternatives such as landfill use where conditions or storage constraints require it. We’ll manage sensitive sites and coordinate application practices to minimize community impact, especially in areas with higher visibility.

<!-- ¶5 -->
OCWUT is transitioning from chlorine gas to OSHG at key facilities. Our team will support this transition by integrating hypochlorite generation systems into daily operations, optimizing dosing strategies, and supporting startup and operator training. By aligning disinfection practices with process control, we’ll reduce variability that can contribute to downstream odor conditions and improve the reliability and acceptance of land application sites.

<!-- ¶6 -->
###### **Fence-Line Performance and Compliance**

<!-- ¶7 -->
We’ll align our approach with OCWUT's performance expectations and Schedule 3 requirements. This includes maintaining dissolved sulfide below 0.5 mg/L at the influent flow at North Canadian, hydrogen sulfide below 10 ppm at the base of all bar screens and belt press units across all facilities, and hydrogen sulfide below 4 ppb at the fence line. We’ll record all analyses and document them at 12-hour intervals in accordance with Schedule 3.

<!-- ¶8 -->
Monitoring locations will represent actual community exposure, including both upwind and downwind conditions. When measurements approach defined thresholds, we will take immediate action through operational adjustments, chemical optimization, or temporary mitigation measures. **This keeps odor control tied to measurable, documented performance.**

<!-- ¶9 -->
When Jacobs took over operations at the City of Waterbury Water Pollution Control Facility, existing odor control systems were in disrepair, and the community was experiencing significant complaints. We conducted an odor study, identified major sources, and implemented a short-term control plan that decreased odor complaints by more than 55% between Year 1 and Year 2. Long-term improvements included a carbon absorber polishing stage, new chemical dosing pumps, replacement of aging air piping, and a $2M odor control systems upgrade completed in partnership with Walsh Engineers.

<!-- ¶10 -->
###### **Rapid Response and Community Engagement**

<!-- ¶11 -->
Odor complaints are a direct signal from the community. **We will respond to all complaints within 1 hour.** Field teams will verify conditions, identify the root cause, and implement corrective actions. **Each complaint will be documented, tracked, and resolved.** Monthly reporting will summarize complaint trends and actions taken, providing transparency and accountability. We do not just log complaints. We resolve them.

<!-- ¶12 -->
###### **Integration with Operations, Maintenance, and Capital Planning**

<!-- ¶13 -->
Odor control is fully integrated into operations, maintenance, and capital planning. It is embedded in process control strategies, maintenance planning, and emergency response procedures. Odor data will also inform capital planning decisions to target improvements where they provide the greatest benefit, including ongoing facility upgrades and new infrastructure coming online through the contract term.

<!-- ¶14 -->
###### **Proven Results**

<!-- ¶15 -->
Across our operations, this approach has consistently reduced odor complaints, improved process stability, and minimized reliance on capital-intensive solutions. **By focusing on process control, maintenance, and targeted optimization, we deliver measurable improvements that communities can see and feel.** These results reflect a repeatable approach that we'll bring to OCWUT from day one.

###### _Phase 2 – Monitoring, Planning, and Process Refinement_

<!-- ¶13 -->
During our site visits, we observed instances where critical instrumentation (DO, flow, and device status) that was inaccurate, disconnected, or not visible in SCADA, directly limiting effective process control. Phase 2 will involve validating each control path end to end (sensor → I/O → PLC logic → communications/historian → HMI), correcting application issues, and implementing verification-based maintenance. The result will be **“SCADA you can trust”—reliable information for risk-informed decisions in routine operations and high-stress wet-weather events** , particularly at North Canadian, where post-event recovery and compliance have been challenging. Trusted data shortens recovery time, protects compliance, and lowers operational cost.

<!-- ¶14 -->
Phase 2 will translate the shared understanding established in Phase 1 into repeatable, dependable day-to-day performance. The objective is to reduce operational variability and risk by clearly:

<!-- ¶15 -->
- <sup>Defining how each facility should operate under all realistic conditions</sup>

<!-- ¶16 -->
- <sup>Ensuring assets and control systems are configured to support those</sup> operating modes

<!-- ¶17 -->
- <sup>Confirming operational decisions are based on trusted, qualified data</sup>

<!-- ¶18 -->
**Process planning, refinement, and monitoring.** Our team will formalize how each process area should operate across normal and non-normal conditions (equipment downtime, construction tie-ins, influent and IPP changes, wet-weather events, chemical adjustments) and align equipment configuration, control narratives, and interlocks with those plans. Where gaps exist, we'll refine control strategies or adjust monitoring elements so operating plans stay executable, resilient, and consistent with OCWUT standards. In parallel, we'll establish data integrity from field device through PLC, SCADA, and operator interface by validating instrument installation, tightening calibration and PM verification routines, and requesting SCADA interface modifications so critical information displays clearly. An equipment anomaly program that ties together operator rounds, PM, SCADA-based condition monitoring, and alarm management will cut nuisance alarms and flag drift earlier.

<!-- ¶19 -->
###### _Phase 3: Process Automation Integration_

<!-- ¶20 -->
In Phase 3, we’ll embed resilient, repeatable automation into daily operations. Using the validated process models, refined control narratives, and qualified data foundation from Phases 1 and 2 will result in stronger permit compliance, better asset reliability, improved resource efficiency, and reduced operator task saturation as outlined in **Exhibit 1-24** . Our site visits showed all facilities running over-aerated, with air production exceeding true process demand—wasted blower runtime, unstable DO profiles, and higher energy cost. Phase 3 will deploy flowand demand-responsive aeration control: accurate air flow measurement, valve and air distribution control, and continuous DO monitoring that make blower output

automatically track real-time treatment needs. The goal is to reduce instances of overproduction and over-aeration and support better kWh/MG performance, lower cost, and more stable biology—directly supporting the RFP's energy management expectations.

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### **Protecting Service, Public Health, and Community Confidence**

<!-- ¶3 -->
Wastewater service can’t pause during an emergency. When something goes wrong, OCWUT needs a partner that will act quickly, communicate clearly, and keep operations under control. We will develop and implement an emergency operating plan that protects public health, supports regulatory compliance, and helps sustain community confidence when conditions are at their most challenging. We will coordinate this plan with OCWUT’s Emergency Response Plan, Flood Emergency Response Plan, and Risk Management Plan; the City’s Emergency Operations Plan; and each facility’s Spill Prevention, Control, and Countermeasure (SPCC) Plan so response actions are aligned before an event occurs. This approach will help OCWUT reduce disruption, protect critical treatment functions, and recover more quickly when emergencies happen.

<!-- ¶4 -->
###### **Prepared, Trained, and Ready To Act**

<!-- ¶5 -->
Strong emergency response starts long before an event occurs. Our facility assessments identified specific vulnerabilities that will inform emergency planning priorities at each site, including flood risk at North Canadian's influent pump station, H2S conditions that restrict access to critical work areas, and redundancy gaps in key process equipment that require defined backup protocols. We will build on OCWUT’s existing procedures and strengthen them with a practical, facilityspecific emergency operating plan tailored to your risks, assets, and operating conditions. Our team will review current emergency procedures and unit process control procedures, confirm emergency equipment availability, and identify critical assets and likely failure points so response efforts stay focused where they matter most.

<!-- ¶6 -->
We will prepare staff through routine drills, tabletop exercises, and scenariobased training covering severe weather, process upsets, power loss, staffing shortages, and other high-consequence events. Ahead of forecasted events, we will stage personnel, equipment, and materials to shorten response time and maintain continuity of operations. We will also maintain agreements for emergency equipment and specialty services so additional resources are available when needed without delay. This preparation will help OCWUT protect permit compliance, maintain treatment continuity, and respond with confidence under pressure.

<!-- ¶4 -->
Our team will operate and maintain OCWUT facilities and utilities under emergency conditions with a plan built around reliability, safety, communication, and continuity of operations. Our emergency operating plan will include staff training, emergency equipment readiness, defined response roles, and coordination procedures with the City and external agencies. –<br>DUNCAN WWTF   2018 OKLAHOMA FLOOD EVENT<br><!-- End of picture text -->

<!-- ¶5 -->
During the September 21, 2018 severe rain event, Duncan WWTF received 7.7 inches of rain in 9 hours, causing flash flooding and influent at the WWTP to peak max of 10,400 GPM for 22 hours.Ahead of this serious storm event, our team implemented the facility’s emergency preparedness and response plan, secured the facility, and procured repair supplies and equipment. When the rain storms hit, we staffed the WWTP overnight to position for immediate emergency response. Flash flooding filled the facility’s yards. To save the pumps, PLC, and electrical systems, our team acted quickly and installed a temporary pump station to continually pump our approximately 200 GPM of rushing water. Our on-site staff and SMEs’ rapid response saved approximately $10K in damages.

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **ENERGY MANAGEMENT – A CORE OPERATING DISCIPLINE**

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### **Understanding OCWUT’s Energy Landscape**

<!-- ¶3 -->
OCWUT's four wastewater treatment facilities and the Witcher Pump Station represent a combined treatment capacity of more than 110 MGD. (Facilityspecific energy profiles, baseline energy intensities, and the operational drivers we observed during site visits are documented in the **Process Control Strategy** section earlier in this Technical Approach.)

<!-- ¶4 -->
OCWUT has made clear that energy management is not optional; it's a contractual obligation. This includes developing and implementing an Energy Efficiency Plan, maintaining electricity usage within +5% of an annually established baseline, meeting with the General Manager annually to audit usage variances and discuss consumption practices, and reporting monthly and annually on energy performance.

<!-- ¶5 -->
OCWUT has confirmed that the Energy Efficiency Plan baseline will be established using the annual average kWh/MG of effluent flow at each facility, a flow-adjusted metric that measures genuine operational efficiency rather than penalizing operators for flow variability outside their control. CIP projects will be considered in baseline development, and OCWUT will remain responsible for providing electricity.

<!-- ¶6 -->
###### EXPERTISE FOR ENERGY OPTIMIZATION AND MORE

<!-- ¶7 -->
Senior energy and chemical efficiency strategist Dawn Lesley, PE, will work alongside the on-site team to turn OCWUT’s baseline-driven energy management into dayto-day results—advising operators, evaluating energy and chemical optimization, and prioritizing the highest-return improvement opportunities.

<!-- ¶8 -->
DAWN LESLEY, PE _O&M ENERGY AND CHEMICAL OPTIMIZATION SME_ Dawn leads energy and chemical optimization across Jacobs’ O&M portfolio, with documented savings in the millions of kWh at comparable plants. She’ll establish defensible facility baselines, train operators on changes that move the kWh/MG needle, and surface low- and no-cost improvements before capital enters the conversation.

<!-- ¶9 -->
We understand these requirements and the

<!-- ¶10 -->
