<!-- ¶11 -->
operational realities behind them. Energy management at OCWUT isn't just about tracking kilowatt hours; it's about managing aging aeration systems, integrating new CIP assets, and making daily process decisions that **keep costs in line without compromising treatment performance or permit compliance** .

<!-- ¶12 -->
###### **Our Approach: Baseline-Driven Energy Management**

<!-- ¶13 -->
Jacobs will **manage energy as a core operating discipline—embedded in daily decision-making, continuously monitored, and fully transparent to OCWUT** . Using historical data, SCADA, and operational trends, we’ll establish a clear, defensible baseline for each facility that reflects actual conditions, seasonal variability, and process loading. From that foundation, we’ll track performance in real time, convert data into actionable insights, and identify opportunities to reduce energy intensity while maintaining process stability, compliance, and system resiliency.

<!-- ¶14 -->
###### _Tracking Performance Where It Counts_

<!-- ¶15 -->
**Our approach focuses on the largest controllable energy drivers, aeration and pumping, where operational discipline has the greatest day-to-day impact.** The facility-by-facility aeration optimization strategy is described in **Process Control Strategy** , and the flow- and demand-responsive aeration control we'll deploy through SCADA is described in the **Phase 3 Automation Integration** subsection of the **SCADA/OT/Cybersecurity** section earlier in this Technical Approach. Proactive maintenance that keeps motors and blowers operating at peak efficiency is detailed in the Maintenance Plan.

<!-- ¶16 -->
###### **Our energy management approach provides the performance tracking**

<!-- ¶17 -->
**framework that ties it together.** Energy performance will be tracked using kWh per million gallons treated, or kWh per thousand pounds of pollutant removed. Chemical disinfection dosing will be tracked using pounds of chemical per million gallons treated. This gives OCWUT transparent reporting, measurable results, and confidence in our commitment to energy and chemical optimization.

<!-- ¶22 -->
µ<sup>**Aeration system optimization**— focusing on air delivery, distribution,</sup> and control strategies to match real-time process needs without overaeration—particularly relevant at North Canadian and Deer Creek where our

## Page 55

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **PUMP STATION OPERATIONS AND SEPTAGE RECEIVING**

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### JACOBS UNDERSTANDS WHY WITCHER PUMP STATION O&M AND SEPTAGE RECEIVING MATTER

<!-- ¶3 -->
The Witcher Pump Station is the critical hydraulic link between Oklahoma City’s collection system and the North Canadian WWTP. Built in 1981 and upgraded in 2012, it operates as a staffed site 12 hours per day, six days per week and is OCWUT’s sole designated septage receiving point, serving licensed haulers across the Oklahoma City service area. Our approach will deliver critical benefits for OCWUT and the community with fewer odor complaints in high-visibility areas, a more defensible biosolids program, and long-term value preserved at the station that ties your collection system to North Canadian.

<!-- ¶4 -->
It directly affects odor at North Canadian, biosolids quality in the land application program, and community trust in the neighborhoods nearest the station.

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### **Pump Station Operations**

<!-- ¶6 -->
###### **Challenges We’ll Address**

<!-- ¶7 -->
We’ll operate the Witcher Pump Station as an integrated extension of the North Canadian treatment process—not simply as a conveyance asset. Our approach centers on three priorities:

<!-- ¶8 -->
**Odor and H₂S.** Our site visits confirmed H₂S-driven corrosion at the influent flow path and odor at the station itself, particularly where flow discharges to the junction box and drops into the gravity line feeding North Canadian. The station’s proximity to a neighboring school, soccer fields, and residential areas makes odor management a high-visibility priority. Current odor masking with Super D provides a short-term measure only

<!-- ¶9 -->
•<sup>**Hydraulic reliability and downstream protection.**We’ll manage wet well levels</sup> and lead/lag pump sequencing to maintain steady flow to North Canadian while minimizing surge. Controlled pump cycling—particularly management of the second pump’s on/off frequency—will reduce force main pressure transients that release H₂S at the downstream junction box. We’ll also test and exercise standby power transfer sequences on a regular schedule to prevent overflow during outages.

<!-- ¶10 -->
**Wet Weather Basin Maintenance.** The three earthen-bottom wet weather basins accumulate grit and settled solids that contribute to odor when basins are activated. The flow equalization basin (FEBs) were cleaned in 2023, and use is currently discouraged due to odor.

<!-- ¶11 -->
•<sup>**Targeted odor reduction at the source.**We’ll optimize the existing ferrous</sup> chloride feed system for H₂S control, focusing on dosing rate and injection point to maximize reaction time within the force main before flow reaches the gravity transition. This is a practical, chemistry-based approach that replaces ineffective masking agents with sulfide reduction you can measure, document, and defend to neighbors. (See our overall odor control approach in the **Odor Control Strategy** section discussed earlier in this Technical Approach.)

<!-- ¶12 -->
**Septage Receiving Rigor.** Site observations noted limited testing of received septage loads beyond pH measurement and visual inspection. Several porta-potty loads were received during our visit. The septage program is regulated under Schedule 6 and tied directly to the biosolids program, so intake discipline has direct downstream consequences.

<!-- ¶13 -->
**Generator Reliability.** We observed that the two generators were leaking antifreeze and likely would not run in that condition. While it did not appear the radiator itself was leaking and this could simply be caused by a loose hose connection, for reliability it requires deeper investigation and inclusion in a preventive maintenance (PM) program.

<!-- ¶14 -->
•<sup>**Condition-based asset stewardship.**The four main station pumps and three</sup> retention pumps are on quarterly and annual vibration analysis schedules, respectively (per Schedule 2). We’ll integrate vibration trending with CMMSdriven PM, generator exercise logs, screen condition tracking, and cathodic protection inspections to maintain high availability and meet Schedule 5 predictive maintenance and PM requirements, protecting OCWUT's capital investment in the hydraulic backbone downstream assets depend on. **See the Maintenance Plan (Assets) section that follows.**

<!-- ¶15 -->
OCWUT-16-26 | O&M OF WASTEWATER FACILITIES AND SOLIDS MANAGEMENT PROGRAM SERVICES  | **50**

## Page 56

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### _Wet Weather Management at Witcher_

<!-- ¶2 -->
When storm flows exceed main station capacity, the retention lift station diverts flow to the three wet weather basins. We’ll coordinate basin activation and drawdown with North Canadian headworks loading to prevent hydraulic shock to the treatment process, protecting downstream permit compliance during storm events, and maintain basin readiness through scheduled drainand-clean cycles per the Annual Drain and Clean Plan (Schedule 15), earthen embankment inspections, and drainage controls that minimize stagnant water and associated odor between storm events.

<!-- ¶3 -->
###### **Septage Receiving Program**

<!-- ¶4 -->
As the sole septage receiving point for Oklahoma City, we’ll take a disciplined approach to operating Witcher station under Schedule 6 , recognizing it directly shapes the quality of your biosolids land application program. We’ll staff a trained attendant at the receiving station during all required hours and execute the process show in **Exhibit 1-27.**

<!-- ¶5 -->
We’ll also enforce hauler compliance with site rules: exterior-only tanker washing, no interior hose use, and water-fill privileges limited to portable toilet haulers under strict hose protocols. Any hauler who fails to comply will have water use suspended. Beyond Schedule 6 compliance, Jacobs will evaluate  septage receiving logs for trends in load characteristics that may contribute to downstream odor or solids quality issues—connecting what enters at Witcher to what ultimately reaches the biosolids program. This data-driven feedback loop will strengthen both the septage program and overall solids management. **See our Sludge and Solids Management Plans** in **Section 5 – Required Plans Submitted with the Proposal** .

## Page 57

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **MAINTENANCE PLAN (ASSETS)**

<!-- ¶2 -->
Jacobs approaches maintenance through an asset management lens— understanding what you have, knowing its condition, and making every dollar count toward long-term reliability, compliance, and safety. Our closed-loop operating model links asset risk, disciplined planning and scheduling, execution quality, and continuous performance feedback, so every maintenance activity is deliberate, traceable, and tied to cost and risk. **The goal isn't just to fix things; it's to prevent failures, extend asset life, and build a program that gets smarter over time.**

<!-- ¶3 -->
We’ve implemented thousands of asset management programs across North America and around the world, and we didn’t just adopt industry best practices; we helped write them. That depth of knowledge is what we’ll bring to OCWUT every day.

<!-- ¶4 -->
###### **What We Found Across the System**

<!-- ¶5 -->
You've set the expectation clearly: every maintenance dollar should protect asset value and extend useful life. Meeting that standard starts with truly understanding what you have. During our March 2026 site visits, technical due diligence, and review of available operating data, we developed a detailed picture of where these facilities stand today, and what it will take to move them from reactive maintenance to sustained reliability. That understanding shapes every element of our plan that follows. Four themes emerged consistently:

<!-- ¶6 -->
•<sup>**H₂S-driven equipment degradation is the most pervasive maintenance**</sup> **challenge across the system.** At North Canadian, hydrogen sulfide concentrations in the headworks, grit building, and solids handling areas are severe enough to prevent personnel access for routine preventive and corrective maintenance. Personal monitors triggered high-level alarms repeatedly during our walkthrough. Electrical systems, HVAC components, structural concrete, and instrumentation throughout the headworks show visible deterioration from prolonged exposure. At Deer Creek, domed primary clarifiers trap H₂S, and the dewatering building cannot be safely accessed during belt filter press operations. At both facilities, odor control systems are largely non-operational—many are obsolete, with original manufacturers no longer providing support. The Witcher Pump Station’s sealed junction box, which receives the force main discharge with no odor control or exhaust, is a suspected major contributor to the concentrated H₂S conditions at North Canadian’s headworks. This isn’t just an odor problem; it’s an access, safety, and asset protection problem that directly limits the maintenance team’s ability to do their job.

<!-- ¶7 -->
- <sup>**The current maintenance program is operating in reactive mode.**Our due</sup> diligence confirmed that maintenance activities have been focused on emergency

<!-- ¶8 -->
162 009385<br><!-- End of picture text -->

<!-- ¶9 -->
###### JACOBS ASSET MANAGEMENT LEADERSHIP

<!-- ¶10 -->
repairs rather than planned prevention. The pattern is visible in cascading failures we documented: grit system downtime at North Canadian leads to primary clarifier bypass, which sends grit into aeration basins and accelerates wear on downstream equipment. Reporting data shows this cycle repeating nearly every month. At Chisholm Creek, both headworks bar screens broke down, a failure with an estimated $200,000 cost for repairs and bypass pumping that due diligence suggests may have been preventable with proactive maintenance. Across the system, the gap between where these facilities are and where they need to be is the gap between firefighting and a disciplined, planned maintenance culture. Jacobs will close that gap.

<!-- ¶11 -->
- <sup>**The existing parts storage and inventory foundation is stronger than we**</sup>

<!-- ¶12 -->
**typically see.** The spare parts inventory is already loaded into NexGen with part numbers, descriptions, locations, and minimum/maximum quantities assigned to individual assets. Parts cages at North Canadian are organized and accessible. This is a foundation we’ll build on rather than replace—preserving institutional knowledge while adding the planning discipline needed to prevent stockouts of critical spares and manage inventory costs.

<!-- ¶13 -->
•<sup>**Significant CIP activity will reshape the maintenance landscape.**South</sup> Canadian’s new 8.5-MGD oxidation ditch, OSHG, and disc thickener are expected online in summer 2026. Deer Creek is scheduled for a 2027 upgrade, including a new blower building, primary clarifier rehabilitation, and additional secondary clarifiers that will increase capacity to 21 MGD, with a further expansion to 27–32 MGD planned for 2032. North Canadian has a new headworks with grit removal in the 2027–2028 CIP plan. These projects will introduce new equipment with active warranties, new process technologies, and new O&M requirements that the maintenance program must absorb seamlessly.

###### **Asset Management System (AMS) — From Framework to Field Results**

<!-- ¶2 -->
A maintenance program without an asset management framework is just a task list. We’ll work closely with OCWUT to develop a structured AMS aligned to ISO 55001 and documented in a Strategic Asset Management Plan (SAMP) that defines policy, roles, decision rights, resources, and implementation timeframes. **We’ll tailor our proven AMS framework, built on the globally recognized model developed by the Institute of Asset Management, to OCWUT’s scope and priorities.** Every element will map concrete deliverables, owners, and governance touchpoints, so the program is auditable, durable, and drives measurable results ( **Exhibit 1-29** ).

<!-- ¶3 -->
Maintenance Manager **Brian Daniels** will lead day-to-day maintenance delivery across all facilities, and Asset Manager **Amy Dembinski, CRL** , will lead development of the AMS and its strategic implementation. **Amy** brings 18 years of CMMS expertise, having **administered more than 100 CMMS databases across Jacobs’ portfolio** , and will be supported by our O&M Asset Management Team.

<!-- ¶4 -->
###### _Governance, Strategy, and Decision Making_

<!-- ¶5 -->
We'll establish a maintenance governance charter that guides OCWUT's asset management strategy and drives development of the Asset Management Policy—defining objectives, decision rights, and change control. The SAMP will set measurable targets for reliability, risk, safety, and cost. During mobilization, we'll complete a baseline condition and criticality assessment across all OCWUT’s assets and produce a risk-ranked register that drives our Maintenance Plan, informs capital improvement planning, and ties staffing and materials strategy to asset risk and workload. Maintenance decisions are driven by a standardized risk model—likelihood of failure and consequence of failure—so resources are consistently prioritized to the assets that matter most.

<!-- ¶21 -->
###### _Drain and Clean Program_

<!-- ¶22 -->
Schedule 15 defines a comprehensive drain and clean scope across all facilities: biannual, triannual, annual, and once-per-5-year-term frequencies for dozens of structures. We’ll develop the Annual Drain and Clean Plan in coordination with OCWUT, integrating each activity into the operations schedule to minimize process disruptions and maximize the inspection and repair opportunities that draindown events create. This plan directly supports PM-1 KPI compliance: 100% completion of the annual Drain and Clean Plan, with a fee at risk per Schedule 5.

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### _Cathodic Protection and Corrosion Control_

<!-- ¶2 -->
We’ll inspect cathodic protection systems per RFP §2.2.3 to verify integrity and operability and apply protective coatings during drain and clean activities to minimize corrosion and extend structural life. Proactive corrosion monitoring is particularly important given the elevated H₂S environment at North Canadian and Deer Creek, where we observed significant concrete deterioration along primary clarifier weirs and structural corrosion in headworks buildings during our site visits.

<!-- ¶3 -->
###### _Building, Grounds, and Housekeeping_

<!-- ¶4 -->
Schedule 3 defines detailed housekeeping standards, and we’ll integrate every one of them into NexGen as scheduled work orders so they’re tracked, measurable, and auditable alongside our mechanical PM program. Key elements include daily interior cleaning and litter removal, bi-weekly floor maintenance, semi-annual window washing and wall cleaning, HVAC system maintenance, pest control, and exterior painting and corrosion touch-up.

<!-- ¶5 -->
For grounds maintenance across the extensive acreage at North Canadian, Deer Creek, and Chisholm Creek, we’ll subcontract lawn and landscaping services based on our assessment that dedicated grounds subcontractors deliver more consistent results than in-house mowing programs at facilities of this scale. South Canadian’s smaller footprint will be maintained in-house. Any housekeeping issues identified during OCWUT’s monthly inspections that remain uncorrected for 30 days trigger fees at risk— **our integrated CMMS tracking prevents items from falling through the cracks by assigning owners, due dates, and escalation triggers to every task.**

<!-- ¶6 -->
###### **Predictive Maintenance (PdM) Program**

<!-- ¶7 -->
PdM catches what PM schedules can’t: the early signs of degradation that happen between scheduled intervals. **We’ll deploy a comprehensive PdM technology suite across OCWUT’s facilities, selectively targeted at equipment based on its criticality and failure modes, ensuring the highest-value equipment receives the most advanced monitoring.**

<!-- ¶8 -->
###### _Vibration Analysis_

<!-- ¶9 -->
We’ll perform quarterly vibration analysis on all large rotating equipment: the four 1,250 HP pumps at Witcher Pump Station, ten blowers (350–800 HP) at North Canadian, South Canadian SBR blowers (and the new oxidation ditch equipment as it comes online), and lift station pumps and blowers at Chisholm Creek and Deer Creek. Annual vibration analysis will cover additional rotating equipment per Schedule 2, including retention station pumps, lift station gear boxes, sand filter pumps, and Phase 3B blowers at North Canadian.

<!-- ¶10 -->
When results identify deficiencies, we’ll open corrective work orders within 5 days (PD-1 KPI). The value of this discipline is tangible: vibration trending on North Canadian’s blowers, for example, will help us detect the mechanical issues that led to the recent blower failure incident, potentially preventing a recurrence.

<!-- ¶11 -->
###### _Electrical Testing_

<!-- ¶12 -->
We’ll perform the full scope of electrical testing defined in Schedule 4 for all substations, high- and medium-voltage equipment, relay testing, and calibration across the quarterly, annual, and 5-year cycles. This includes transformer inspections, switchgear and capacitor maintenance, 4160V motor

<!-- ¶1 -->

Specialty high-voltage electrical testing beyond on-site staff capabilities, including work covered under Schedule 11, will be performed by our qualified subcontractors and fully integrated into NexGen for tracking and follow-up. <!-- ¶2 -->
###### _Full PdM Technology Suite_

<!-- ¶3 -->
Beyond vibration and electrical testing, our PdM program includes motor signature analysis to detect rotor and stator faults, particularly valuable for the aging motor fleet at North Canadian, along with ultrasound testing for leak detection, bearing lubrication monitoring, and electrical discharge detection; infrared thermography to identify abnormal heat patterns in electrical and mechanical systems; and oil analysis to evaluate lubricant condition and contamination for early wear detection.

<!-- ¶4 -->
All PdM findings feed directly into NexGen. Deficiencies identified through any predictive testing method will be addressed within 60 days (PD-5 KPI). Over time, trending PdM data will refine PM frequencies and justify targeted investments in monitoring technology where the return avoided downtime is clear.

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### **Corrective Maintenance Program**

<!-- ¶6 -->
**Disciplined corrective maintenance isn’t just about fixing what’s broken; it’s about preventing cascading consequences and building the data that makes the entire program smarter over time.**

<!-- ¶7 -->
At North Canadian, we’ve documented a pattern that illustrates why this matters: grit system downtime leads to primary clarifier bypass, which sends grit into aeration basins, accelerates wear on clarifier drive mechanisms and downstream pumping equipment, and creates conditions for compliance events. Reporting data shows this cycle repeating nearly every month. That pattern is the direct result of reactive maintenance, and it’s exactly the kind of failure chain a structured corrective program breaks by addressing root causes, not just symptoms.

<!-- ¶8 -->
**Every corrective maintenance event will be tracked in NexGen with full documentation: failure mode, root cause, and operational impact recorded on every work order.** We’ll apply Failure Mode and Effects Analysis to systematically eliminate repeat failures at the root cause. Each corrective event becomes an input to improve the overall maintenance program by adjusting PM frequencies, targeting PdM investments, or escalating chronic issues to capital planning. The result: corrective maintenance runs as a controlled process, not a reactive response. Our response and closure discipline is aligned with Schedule 5 KPIs: corrective work

<!-- ¶9 -->
orders opened within 5 days of deficiency identification (CM-1), 100% of corrective work orders completed and closed within 60 days (CM-2), and Critical Assets returned to service within 2 days of failure (CM-3).

<!-- ¶10 -->
Facility-specific corrective maintenance priorities identified through our due diligence:

<!-- ¶11 -->
- <sup>**North Canadian:**Grit system reliability, RAS pump availability (Phase 1 screw</sup> pump currently OOS), blower maintenance and condition monitoring following recent failure incident, H₂S-related equipment degradation across headworks, solids handling, and HVAC systems

<!-- ¶12 -->
- <sup>**Deer Creek:**Blower availability maintenance pending 2027 CIP, secondary</sup> clarifier mechanism issues, sand filter blower rehabilitation, algae management on secondary clarifiers

<!-- ¶13 -->
- <sup>**Chisholm Creek:**Bar screen reliability program, grit system evaluation and</sup> coordination with OCWUT to resolve design issues

<!-- ¶14 -->
- <sup>**South Canadian:**Maintaining SBR operability through CIP transition,</sup> commissioning support for new oxidation ditch equipment

<!-- ¶15 -->
- <sup>**System-wide:**Odor control equipment restoration, overhead crane inspections,</sup> equipment shaft guard replacements, generator reliability

<!-- ¶16 -->
###### **Maintenance Staffing Approach**

<!-- ¶17 -->
We will staff each facility with dedicated maintenance teams sized for that facility’s complexity and workload. Daily preventive and routine corrective maintenance will be performed by personnel assigned to each facility, not shared across distant sites. South Canadian’s geographic distance from the northern facilities (45–50 minutes) and its new equipment profile demand a dedicated on-site team focused on warranty compliance, commissioning support, and a PM-heavy maintenance ratio from day one. The northern facilities—North Canadian, Deer Creek, and Chisholm Creek—will share specialized resources for electrical, I&C, and heavy corrective maintenance through a coordinated model that takes advantage of their relative proximity.

<!-- ¶18 -->
We're **also restructuring how drain and clean work gets done** . Operators—not mechanics—will execute the Schedule 15 drain and clean program, with mechanics following on for post-clean inspections and any preventive maintenance the drain-down reveals. We've sized the operations workforce specifically for this new approach, adding hourly operator capacity at every facility (detailed in our Staffing and Training Plan in Section 5 – Required Plans). The result for OCWUT: more mechanic wrench time on PM, PdM, and corrective work that protects long-term asset value and operational reliability.

<!-- ¶18 -->
###### **Warranty Management**

<!-- ¶19 -->
With South Canadian’s new oxidation ditch, hypochlorite generation system, disc thickener, and cloth filtration expected online in summer 2026, and Deer Creek’s major CIP upgrade planned for 2027, warranty management will be a critical focus from the start of this contract.

<!-- ¶20 -->
What makes this a genuine differentiator is how the team is structured: regional maintenance specialists are separate and standalone from our project teams. They don't draw resources away from other projects when OCWUT needs support, and other projects don't draw them away from OCWUT. They're Jacobs personnel who will know your facilities, your CMMS, and your safety protocols— and they're available without the procurement delays and onboarding friction that come with third-party contractors. Unlike typical operators, we combine on-site delivery with regional and national support structures, bringing specialized expertise, benchmarking, and proven practices from more than 100 Jacobs-operated facilities.

<!-- ¶21 -->
We’ll maintain a comprehensive warranty register within NexGen, tracking warranty terms, expiration dates, coverage conditions, and required maintenance actions for every applicable asset. NexGen will flag assets under warranty when corrective maintenance is requested, triggering a review of warranty applicability before any repair or replacement work proceeds. PM tasks required to maintain warranty coverage will be built into the PM schedule as non-negotiable items—because a voided warranty doesn’t just cost money; it transfers risk from the manufacturer to the operator.

<!-- ¶22 -->
Monthly reports will include an updated warranty list per Service Agreement §3.03(d). At South Canadian, the volume of new CIP equipment will require close

coordination with manufacturers, system integrators, and construction contractors during commissioning to document warranty start dates, coverage parameters, and required maintenance protocols. <!-- ¶2 -->
###### **Inventory Management and Spare Parts**

<!-- ¶3 -->
We will manage OCWUT’s spare parts inventory through NexGen, building on the existing well-organized system already loaded into the platform with part numbers, descriptions, locations, and minimum/maximum quantities assigned to individual assets. A dedicated Stores/Parts Manager will maintain inventory integrity by receiving, cataloging, issuing, cycle counting, and reordering.

<!-- ¶4 -->
Our critical spare parts strategy starts with the Schedule 20 definition of Critical Assets and maps their failure-prone components to a stocking plan that supports the 2-day return-to-service requirement (CM-3). Inventory levels will be aligned with Minimum Inventory Levels compliance requirements and verified through regular cycle counts. Automated reorder triggers within NexGen based on minimum/maximum levels will prevent stockouts while controlling carrying costs. Bills of material linked to each asset will enable efficient parts identification during corrective events.

<!-- ¶5 -->
Parts and supplies to be paid for from the M&R Fund will be inventoried with detailed justifications and General Manager approval per Schedule 20. All inventory is the property of OCWUT.

<!-- ¶6 -->
###### **Maintenance and Repair (M&R) Fund and Rehabilitation and Replacement (R&R) Fund Management**

<!-- ¶7 -->
We’ll manage the tiered M&R Fund structure with the precision of documentation that OCWUT’s contract demands:

<!-- ¶8 -->
- <sup>**Level A (≤$10,000):**Parts and supplies reimbursed without prior Service</sup> Approval Form (SAF); labor at Jacobs’ expense, including subcontractors and overtime. Documentation submitted weekly and included in the Monthly Payment Request.

<!-- ¶9 -->
- <sup>**Level B ($10,000–$50,000):**Requires an executed SAF prior to ordering</sup> materials. OCWUT pays labor costs including subcontractors.

<!-- ¶10 -->
- <sup>**Level C (>$50,000):**OCWUT competitively procures; Jacobs cooperates fully with</sup> procurement and installation coordination.

<!-- ¶11 -->
Each year, we will develop and submit the annual M&R budget, aligning with the condition assessment cycle and capital planning process. We’ll manage expenditures within 5% of the approved annual budget to avoid unrecoverable costs, a discipline that requires real-time fund tracking in NexGen with automated alerts as spending approaches thresholds. We understand that the R&R Fund addresses single asset rehabilitations or replacements exceeding $10,000 that substantially extend service life by 10 or more years. An executed SAF is required before procurement.

<!-- ¶12 -->
**We build documentation discipline into every maintenance activity — not because the contract requires it, but because it's how we protect the assets and the program's financial integrity.** Every PM completion, every inspection result, every corrective action is recorded in NexGen with timestamps, photographic evidence, and clear accountability. When a piece of equipment reaches end of life, the maintenance record speaks for itself by demonstrating the care that went into extending its service and supporting a straightforward path to M&R Fund reimbursement. That same data trail feeds our condition assessments, informs capital planning recommendations, and gives OCWUT full transparency into how maintenance dollars are being spent. This level of documentation ensures every maintenance dollar is traceable, justified, and aligned to asset condition, risk, and lifecycle decision-making.

<!-- ¶13 -->
###### **On-Call and Emergency Maintenance Response**

<!-- ¶14 -->
We’ll maintain an on-call maintenance rotation to address after-hours equipment issues and emergency situations: one mechanic and one electrician on call per week for the northern facilities group (North Canadian, Deer Creek, Chisholm Creek), with a separate mechanic rotation for South Canadian. On-call staff will be facility-familiar personnel drawn from the existing dedicated teams, rotating responsibilities to distribute workload equally.

<!-- ¶15 -->
The Critical Asset 2-day return-to-service requirement (CM-3) is backed by Jacobs’ three layers of response: on-site critical spare parts inventory stocked specifically for high-risk equipment, on-call staff availability for immediate mobilization, and Regional Maintenance Team support for specialized or heavy lift work that exceeds the on-site team’s capability. Emergency events—severe weather, flooding, and equipment failure cascades—will trigger enhanced staffing protocols coordinated between operations and maintenance leadership, with clear escalation paths and communication procedures documented in our Emergency Response Plan.

<!-- ¶6 -->
###### _Maintenance of Plant Operations (MOPO)_

<!-- ¶7 -->
MOPO is the highest-risk operational element of any capital project at a working wastewater facility. We treat MOPO as a living document that evolves through each design phase, not a static plan developed once and filed away. We’ll identify critical process interdependencies and capacity constraints before construction begins, sequence construction activities to maintain treatment capability and permit compliance, develop contingency plans for equipment outages including temporary process configurations, and coordinate shutdown windows with seasonal flow patterns, wet weather risk, and OCWUT’s operational priorities.

<!-- ¶8 -->
Jacobs remains responsible for meeting all Schedule 5 Performance Requirements during CIP construction. That accountability is why MOPO planning starts at 35% design and receives dedicated attention at every subsequent phase, and why the CIP Construction Manager coordinates directly with operations leadership to avoid conflicts between construction schedules and treatment obligations.

<!-- ¶9 -->
###### _The Jacobs Advantage: Integrated O&M and Engineering_

<!-- ¶10 -->
**As both an O&M operator and one of the world’s largest engineering and construction firms, Jacobs brings a perspective to CIP support that pure-play operators can’t match.** Our operators bring real-world insight into design workshops, constructability reviews, and commissioning—identifying issues that look fine on paper but won’t work in the field. At the Wilmington, Delaware Wastewater Treatment Facility, our operators proposed resequencing a multi-phase aeration upgrade to work on two basins simultaneously instead of one. **Our process engineers verified compliance could be maintained, and the result was a 40% reduction in the construction schedule and 20% cost savings.** For OCWUT, this means CIP projects that are practical to operate from day one, resilient under real-world conditions, and designed with the people who run them every day.

<!-- ¶11 -->

###### **FROM PROCESS CHALLENGE TO RELIABLE BIOSOLIDS PERFORMANCE**

<!-- ¶9 -->
In Vancouver, Jacobs turned biosolids processing into a dependable, high-performing operation. Through a strong O&M program, the plant processed 100% of the biosolids it produced through the incinerator year over year, excluding only planned diversions during annual outages. The team also drove measurable efficiency gains, including a recent 20% reduction in polymer costs by bypassing the Mannich system for emulsion totes. To strengthen reliability and control across the facility, Jacobs also leveraged broader technical resources to implement a plantwide SCADA and PLC upgrade with new automated programming—helping improve performance, reduce manual intervention, and keep this critical process under control.

<!-- ¶10 -->

By mobilizing an experienced on-site team supported by regional specialists and implementing a phased recovery strategy,

###### **ODOR CONTROL**

<!-- ¶6 -->
Odor control is one of the most visible performance measures in wastewater operations. Jacobs brings experience across the full odor control life cycle, including formal odor studies, dispersion modeling, operational improvements, capital upgrades, and ongoing fence line monitoring.

<!-- ¶7 -->
We follow a consistent framework of field sampling and source identification first, dispersion modeling to quantify off-site impact, operational improvement where possible, and capital recommendations sized to deliver the most community benefit per dollar invested. Operators conduct routine H2S monitoring using portable meters, make scheduled fence line patrols during high-odor seasons, and follow documented corrective action protocols when readings exceed thresholds.

<!-- ¶8 -->
**At Waterbury, Connecticut, Jacobs reduced public odor complaints by more than 55% within the first contract year** through a formal source characterization study and a structured two-track response: immediate operational controls followed by a **$2M capital upgrade to chemical scrubber systems** .

<!-- ¶9 -->
**Jacobs manages odor and air quality compliance across two WWTPs with an active biosolids incinerator in Vancouver, Washington** , using Continuous Emissions Monitoring (CEM) systems to track regulated parameters in real time under a Title V air permit. We delivered capital improvements to odor control

<!-- ¶10 -->
systems at both plants as part of our integrated capital management program alongside a $500,000 heat exchanger investment that extended the incinerator's operational life, deferred an unplanned shutdown, and eliminated associated confined space and odor risks.

<!-- ¶11 -->
Jacobs designed and operates an advanced odor control system in **Agua Nueva, Arizona, that removes virtually all facility odors without additional chemicals and with minimal maintenance requirements** . We achieved this deliberate design outcome by embedding operations expertise into the facility's engineering from the start.

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### **MAINTENANCE**

<!-- ¶6 -->
Jacobs has implemented more than 100 asset management programs across North America, and our approach is built on a straightforward progression: get preventive maintenance locked in first, layer in predictive technologies (i.e., vibration analysis, motor signature analysis, infrared thermography, ultrasound, and oil analysis), and use the data to move toward condition-based decisions rather than fixed-interval assumptions. Corrective work orders open within 24 hours; we target closure within 30 days.

<!-- ¶7 -->
NexGen, being implemented now through Jacobs consulting, gives OCWUT full visibility into work order status, asset history, and maintenance costs. Our Asset Replacement Modeling (ARM) tool uses condition scores, asset age, and maintenance history to support capital planning decisions.

<!-- ¶8 -->
When Jacobs assumed operations of **Baton Rouge's North and South WWTPs** in 2023, the facilities faced a significant backlog of deferred maintenance, repair, and replacement needs. We immediately conducted a detailed inventory and condition assessment, developed targeted strategies to address critical equipment deficiencies, and launched a rigorous belt filter press review and repair program, **bringing the presses back to full effectiveness and significantly improving the system's capacity to handle biosolids** . We also conducted a comprehensive facility condition assessment and capital repair and replacement plan, aligning recommendations with the City's funding availability.

###### **CONTRACT COMPLIANCE**

<!-- ¶6 -->
**Jacobs maintains a 99.98% compliance rate across our O&M portfolio. This record is reflected in Waterbury's 99.57% permit compliance rate over 5 years, Vancouver's back-to-back NACWA Platinum Peak Performance Award, and consecutive Outstanding Performance recognitions from the Washington Department of Ecology.** Behind those numbers is a structured program: standardized standard operating procedures (SOPs), rigorous laboratory management, and a dedicated team of compliance specialists supporting on-site staff at every Jacobs-operated facility nationwide.

<!-- ¶7 -->
Every Jacobs project is audited on a structured cycle within 6 months of contract start, with frequency adjusted based on performance. Audit teams work through 70 specific topics against contractual and regulatory requirements, and findings go to the project manager with a 30/60/90-day action timeline. We'll bring that same approach here. **Jeff Heroux** serves as our compliance lead on this project. As Director of Compliance and Regulatory Affairs, Jeff **provides oversight across all Jacobs O&M contracts and brings a dedicated team of 20 compliance specialists** .

David will serve as OCWUT’s Project Manager and onsite representative, with overall responsibility for safe, compliant, and reliable operation of the County’s wastewater facilities. He has 30 years of water and wastewater operations leadership, including direct experience managing the Oklahoma City wastewater system, multi-facility operations with peak flows up to 250 MGD (combined), and teams of more than 80 staff. David has a proven record of restoring compliance, strengthening maintenance performance through CMMS-driven programs, improving operational reliability, and leading workforce development in complex utility environments. His experience positions him to provide day-one leadership, maintain 24/7 operational continuity, and serve as a responsive liaison to the General Manager or designee while advancing long-term system performance.

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **SECTION 4**

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### **JACOBS IN OKLAHOMA: A 30-YEAR TRACK RECORD WITH THE OKLAHOMA DEPARTMENT OF ENVIRONMENTAL QUALITY**

<!-- ¶3 -->
Our team has 30 years of experience working with the Oklahoma Department of Environmental Quality (ODEQ), supporting land application programs, and maintaining compliance across Oklahoma as biosolids regulations have changed. The City of Duncan provides the clearest demonstration of our relationship with ODEQ and our Oklahoma regulatory experience. **Since 1995, Jacobs has provided full-service operations and maintenance for the City of Duncan's wastewater infrastructure under ODEQ oversight.** Our scope at Duncan mirrors OCWUT's in similar ways: full-service WWTP operations, compliance sampling, laboratory services, biosolids handling, and a land application reuse program managing effluent across 38 irrigation pivot sites. That sustained record of compliance and operational performance earned the Duncan WWTP the **Oklahoma Water Environment Association's Municipal Plant of the Year award in 2025** .

<!-- ¶4 -->
During the 2018 catastrophic flooding event, **we kept the Duncan WWTP operational by repurposing equipment, securing mobile systems, and coordinating closely with ODEQ** . That's the kind of regulatory relationship that matters most when conditions are most challenging.

<!-- ¶5 -->
Since the start of the contract, we’ve also supported the City of Duncan with their Industrial Pretreatment Program (IPP). In 2025, when a permitted industry raised questions about revised limits stemming from our updated Technically Based Local Limits and Sewer Use Ordinance work, we engaged ODEQ directly on the City's behalf. **ODEQ confirmed our reasoning was sound, expressed confidence in us as the Control Authority representative, and commended the quality of our permit documentation** .

<!-- ¶6 -->
Beyond compliance, we’ve also delivered nearly **$5M in cost and energy savings for the City through targeted process improvements and operational optimization** , demonstrating that regulatory compliance and operational performance are not competing priorities.

<!-- ¶7 -->
###### <mark>WHAT THIS MEANS FOR OCWUT</mark>

<!-- ¶8 -->
Oklahoma’s regulatory environment has evolved significantly over the past 3 decades, and we have been actively engaged throughout that time. **Our Oklahoma-based engineering staff work directly with ODEQ on construction permitting and operational compliance** . ODEQ Coordination and Community Engagement Lead **Arya Simon** brings 8 years of experience as a Construction Permitting Engineer in ODEQ's Water Quality Division followed by a year with EPA's Clean Water State Revolving Fund, giving her an insider's understanding of Oklahoma's regulatory process to keep projects moving through agency review. This firsthand knowledge of agency processes, regulatory expectations, and staff coordination enables us to anticipate reviews, streamline permitting, and resolve agency comments efficiently.

<!-- ¶9 -->
We routinely prepare permit applications, support inspections, and maintain compliance documentation, guiding projects from planning through approval and operation. **Our long-standing relationships with ODEQ and deep understanding of Oklahoma’s regulatory framework help reduce project risk, avoid delays, and ensure consistent compliance** .

<!-- ¶10 -->
At OCWUT, we’ll bring that same disciplined approach: permit requirements incorporated into daily operations, Oklahoma Pollutant Discharge Elimination System (OPDES) Discharge Monitoring Reports delivered to OCWUT at least 3 business days before regulatory due dates, complete and defensible records maintained for inspections and audits, and proactive communication with ODEQ when conditions change. When ODEQ conducts inspections, we’re prepared. When reporting is due, it is accurate and on time. And as the regulatory landscape shifts, particularly on biosolids, we’ll position OCWUT to respond.

<!-- ¶11 -->
That is not the framework we’re proposing to build. _It is how we already operate in Oklahoma every day._

<!-- ¶12 -->

When we led Duncan’s disinfection upgrade, we partnered with ODEQ to convert Outfall 001 to gravity flow, eliminate chronic high-flow constraints, and negotiate less stringent cBOD limits—delivering a more reliable discharge system, reduced operational risk, and a permit that reflects real facility performance.

<!-- ¶4 -->
###### **OKLAHOMA LAW: REGULATORY KNOWLEDGE AND ENVIRONMENTAL COMPLIANCE**

<!-- ¶5 -->
Operating wastewater facilities in Oklahoma requires more than knowing permit limits. It requires understanding how federal, state, and local regulations translate into daily operational decisions and applying that knowledge consistently across every facility. We’ll bring that hands-on understanding and apply it from day one at OCWUT.

<!-- ¶6 -->
OCWUT's facilities operate within a layered regulatory framework that our team knows well. At the federal level, this includes the Clean Water Act, Clean Air Act, National Pollutant Discharge Elimination System permitting requirements, and biosolids management standards under 40 CFR Part 503, along with applicable stormwater and waste management standards.

<!-- ¶7 -->
In Oklahoma, these requirements are implemented through ODEQ under the OPDES program. The specific administrative rules governing OCWUT's operations include:

<!-- ¶8 -->
- <sup>**OAC 252:606**: OPDES permitting, reporting, and compliance requirements</sup>

<!-- ¶9 -->
- <sup>**OAC 252:616**: Biosolids management standards applicable to Class B treatment,</sup> land application, and documentation

<!-- ¶10 -->
- <sup>**OAC 252:656**: Laboratory accreditation and certification requirements</sup>

<!-- ¶11 -->
- <sup>**OAC 252:515**: Operator certification requirements governing</sup> staffing qualifications

<!-- ¶12 -->
- <sup>**OAC 252:100**: Air pollution control regulations covering emergency generators</sup>

<!-- ¶13 -->
- •<sup>**OAC 252:656 & 627**: Water reuse</sup>

<!-- ¶14 -->
###### **RISK MANAGEMENT, PROCESS SAFETY, AND ENVIRONMENTAL EMERGENCY PROGRAMS**

<!-- ¶15 -->
The North Canadian facility operates under both a **Risk Management Plan (RMP)** and **Process Safety Management (PSM)** requirements, given the presence of chemicals that trigger these thresholds. We'll maintain current RMP/ PSM documentation, conduct required program updates, and coordinate PSM compliance as an integrated component of the facility safety program.

<!-- ¶16 -->
At each facility, we’ll maintain a current **Spill Prevention, Control, and Countermeasure (SPCC) Plan** , updated as required and integrated with the overall Emergency Operating Plan and the facility's day-to-day operational protocols.

<!-- ¶17 -->
**We’ll complete and submit annually by March 1 Tier II Emergency and Hazardous Chemical Inventory** forms for North Canadian, South Canadian, Deer Creek, and Witcher Pump Station—one of those firm, non-negotiable deadlines our team tracks and executes without prompting.

<!-- ¶18 -->
We’ll align emergency response planning with OCWUT's Emergency Response Plan and the City of Oklahoma City's Emergency Operations Plan, with coordination across the Flood Emergency Response Plan, RMP, and SPCC Plan.

<!-- ¶19 -->
###### **OKLAHOMA CORPORATION COMMISSION (OCC)— PETROLEUM STORAGE TANKS**

<!-- ¶20 -->
OCWUT's facilities include underground and aboveground petroleum storage tanks at North Canadian, South Canadian, and Chisholm Creek, all subject to OCC's Petroleum Storage Tank Program. These tanks used for regular unleaded and diesel fuel require active permit certification, routine inspection compliance, and meticulous recordkeeping under OCC regulations.

<!-- ¶21 -->
**We'll maintain current Petroleum Storage Tank Program Permit Certifications for all three facilities and integrate underground storage tank inspection and compliance activities** into our facility management protocols. We’ll incorporate spill prevention measures for these tanks into each facility's **SPCC Plan** , creating a unified approach to petroleum release prevention across the portfolio.

<!-- ¶22 -->
We understand how these regulations translate into sampling frequencies, process control targets, record-keeping standards, and reporting timelines, and we incorporate those requirements directly into plant operations, not as an afterthought.

## Page 126

<!-- ¶1 -->
Among these proposals, House Bill 3403 has emerged as the most substantive and actively advancing measure. House Bill 3403 establishes a 3-year biosolids research pilot program administered by the ODEQ and Oklahoma State University, with defined post-pilot outcomes that could result in continued land application or a phased reduction and potential statewide prohibition beginning in 2029. Notably, the bill includes a potential requirement for incremental reductions in land-applied biosolids, effectively mandating reductions of up to 25 percent per year, culminating in a full cessation plan by December 2029 if adverse findings are reached. As a result, House Bill 3403 currently represents the most likely near-term policy change that could materially affect OCWUT’s solids management strategy.

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### **STORMWATER**

<!-- ¶3 -->
We'll prepare, administer, and maintain OPDES Stormwater Discharge Permits for each facility. Our team understands the distinction between OCWUT's and the Proposer's respective responsibilities under Schedule 12—OCWUT executes and submits; we prepare, obtain approvals, and administer—and we'll manage that division of responsibility clearly.

<!-- ¶4 -->
###### **EMERGING REGULATORY RISK: OKLAHOMA'S BIOSOLIDS LEGISLATION**

<!-- ¶5 -->
**One of the most significant regulatory developments affecting OCWUT’s program is the continued legislative effort in Oklahoma to regulate, restrict, or potentially prohibit the land application of biosolids** , building on the momentum that began with Senate Bill 3 during the 2025 session. In the 2026 legislative session, multiple measures have been introduced that reflect this ongoing push, including House Bill 3411, Senate Bills 1799, 1818, and 2141. Collectively, these bills signal sustained legislative scrutiny of biosolids management through expanded PFAS testing and disclosure requirements, increased transparency and public notification, tighter application restrictions, and, in some cases, outright prohibition.

<!-- ¶6 -->

###### **STAFFING AND TRAINING PLAN**

<!-- ¶4 -->
OCWUT’s facilities demand reliable 24/7 operations, rigorous regulatory compliance, proactive asset stewardship, and a workforce that performs with excellence under both normal and emergency conditions. Meeting these demands will start with bringing in experienced leaders, qualified operators, and maintenance technicians and implementing a support structure that prevents gaps before they become problems.

<!-- ¶5 -->
Our staffing and training plan, which we developed in accordance with Schedule 10, will **deliver a stable, well-qualified workforce backed by structured training, cross-training across facilities and functions, on-call coverage, and regional resources** . Our plan reflects what we learned during our site visits to all five OCWUT facilities, analysis of the RFP and Service Agreement, and decades of experience staffing and managing multi-site wastewater O&M contracts of similar size and complexity across the country. The result will be **dependable operations without service gaps** and a team culture where people take pride in their work, stay for the long term, and grow.

<!-- ¶6 -->

**Strong O&M performance begins with strong leadership.** We have assembled a leadership team with deep experience operating multi-facility wastewater systems, managing 24/7 workforces, and building highperforming cultures, because the caliber of leadership will directly shape the quality of daily operations, staff retention, and responsiveness to OCWUT. Project Manager **David Pitocchelli and our leadership team** will set the tone for a culture of accountability, responsiveness, and continuous improvement. They’ve done this before at complex, multi-facility systems, and they’ll bring that same leadership presence and discipline to OCWUT from day one.

###### **Staffing Approach and Facility Coverage**

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### _Staffing Model_

<!-- ¶3 -->
We will deploy a workforce sized and structured to operate all OCWUT facilities safely, compliantly, and without interruption, as summarized in **Exhibit 5-3** . Our staffing approach will be built on three principles: **retain qualified incumbent staff, right-size coverage for each facility’s operational complexity, and build resilience so that vacancies, absences, and emergencies don’t compromise performance.**

<!-- ¶4 -->
We will retain qualified staff currently working at the facilities who wish to continue, preserving institutional knowledge and operational continuity. Details of our employee transition approach are provided later in this section under our **Draft Transition Plan** . We’ll recruit for any positions not filled through retention through our dedicated resource planning group, local hiring networks, and national O&M talent pipeline (see discussion on these later in this section).

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### _Fewer Managers, More Doers_

<!-- ¶6 -->
**A staffing model built around wrench time and process control.** We’ve intentionally restructured the workforce mix from what’s in place today, shifting investment out of management overhead and into the operators and technicians who keep the plants running. The result is a leaner leadership layer paired with the hands-on capacity needed to meet OCWUT’s drain-and-clean, process control, and 24/7 coverage expectations as illustrate in below.

- <sup>**More wrench time for mechanics.**Reassigning drain-and-clean to operators</sup> frees the maintenance team to focus on preventive, predictive, and corrective maintenance that keeps assets reliable and reduces unplanned breakdowns. <!-- ¶4 -->
###### _24/7 Facility Coverage_

<!-- ¶5 -->
North Canadian, Deer Creek, Chisholm Creek, and South Canadian will each be staffed 24 hours a day, 7 days a week, 365 days a year with qualified operators. Our shift structure will use 8-hour shifts organized into overlapping groups to provide complete coverage while managing overtime costs and maintaining staff wellbeing. This model has proven effective at our similarly sized multi-facility contracts, where it provides adequate overlap for shift exchanges, sampling continuity, and communication between crews.

<!-- ¶6 -->
**We’ll maintain day, night, weekend, and holiday coverage through a structured relief rotation.** We’ll stagger supervisory coverage to extend leadership presence across shifts. During vacations, sick leave, and other planned or unplanned absences, we’ll maintain coverage through trained relief operators, cross-qualified staff from other facilities, and if needed, regional bench resources deployed on short notice.

<!-- ¶7 -->
In the final staffing and training plan, we will provide detailed shift schedule tables, which are too lengthy to provide in this proposal. They will reflect the shift schedules described above.

<!-- ¶8 -->
###### _On-Call Rotation and Emergency Response Staffing_

<!-- ¶9 -->
We will implement an on-call rotation covering operations, maintenance, instrumentation and controls, and management escalation to keep all OCWUT facilities staffed with properly trained and qualified personnel at all times (Schedule 10). The on-call structure will provide:

<!-- ¶10 -->
- <sup>**Operations coverage**with designated on-call operators for each facility, rotating</sup> on a defined schedule to prevent fatigue and maintain readiness

<!-- ¶11 -->
- <sup>**Maintenance and I&C/electrical response**with on-call technicians available to</sup> respond to equipment failures, SCADA alarms, and instrumentation issues

<!-- ¶12 -->
- <sup>**Management escalation**so that the project manager, assistant project manager,</sup> or designated manager will always be reachable for rapid decision making

<!-- ¶13 -->
•<sup>**Backup escalation**if the first-call</sup> responder is unavailable, with a clear chain of contact and response-time expectations This on-call structure, illustrated in **Exhibit 5-4** , **will support rapid, confident response to weather events, process upsets, power outages, odor events, equipment failures, and other unplanned conditions** —protecting OCWUT’s compliance, assets, and community.

<!-- ¶10 -->
###### **Vacancy Management, Retention, and Staffing Resiliency**

<!-- ¶11 -->
OCWUT’s service agreement is clear: inability to fill vacancies is not an excuse for failing to meet performance requirements. We take that seriously, and it dovetails with our overarching staffing philosophy. We’ll design our staffing model to prevent vacancies before they occur, fill them quickly when they do, and maintain full operational coverage in the interim.

<!-- ¶12 -->
###### _Preventing Vacancies: Train to Retain_

<!-- ¶13 -->
The most effective vacancy strategy is keeping good people. Our retention approach will start with competitive compensation and benefits, then layer on career development, recognition, a positive culture, and the kind of leadership that makes people want to stay. This includes:

<!-- ¶14 -->
- <sup>**Career advancement pathways**from entry-level through licensed operator,</sup> specialized maintenance, and supervisory positions, giving every employee a clear line of sight to growth

<!-- ¶15 -->
- <sup>**Certification incentives**that cover exam fees and preparation costs and provide</sup> promotions and bonuses for achieving advanced certifications (details in the Licensing and Certification subsection)

<!-- ¶16 -->
- <sup>**Leadership development**through Jacobs University, including workshops on</sup> communication, coaching, team performance, and management fundamentals

<!-- ¶17 -->
- _Filling Vacancies Promptly_

<!-- ¶18 -->
When vacancies do occur, our dedicated resource planning group, which is focused exclusively on O&M recruiting, will act immediately. Over the past year, their efforts have doubled new hires across our O&M portfolio, reduced turnover, and promoted high-performing staff into new roles. Our vacancy management approach includes:

<!-- ¶19 -->
- <sup>**Immediate posting and recruiting**through our centralized recruiting system,</sup> local job networks, veteran hiring programs, and targeted social media outreach

<!-- ¶20 -->
- <sup>**Internal mobility**including offering open positions first to qualified Jacobs</sup> O&M staff across our national portfolio, many of whom view Oklahoma City as a desirable location

<!-- ¶21 -->

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **SLUDGE MANAGEMENT PLAN**

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### **Why Sludge Management Matters to OCWUT**

<!-- ¶3 -->
Sludge management is one of the daily responsibilities in this contract with the highest stakes. When it runs well, it’s invisible: storage stays controlled, hauling stays on schedule, land application stays compliant, and the community doesn’t think twice about it. When it doesn’t, problems compound quickly. Backlog builds on the pad, odor complaints follow, and compliance exposure grows.

<!-- ¶4 -->
**OCWUT's program does not need reactive management. It needs a partner who treats solids as a planned, coordinated operational function from day one.**

<!-- ¶5 -->
Jacobs will manage sludge from generation through final use or disposal as one fully integrated program, connecting process control, dewatering, storage, odor management, hauling, land application, and compliance reporting into a coordinated system that stays ahead of problems rather than chasing them. This Sludge Management Plan addresses Schedule 14 requirements directly and aligns with our Solids Management Plan, Odor Control Plan, and Process Control Plan. Solids, odor, and process performance do not operate independently. We manage them together.

<!-- ¶6 -->
###### **Understanding OCWUT’s Existing Program**

<!-- ¶7 -->
OCWUT operates an established and regulated sludge and biosolids program that requires daily coordination and disciplined execution across four facilities, approximately 170 permitted land application sites, and roughly 13,500 acres.

<!-- ¶8 -->
ODEQ-approved Sludge Management Plans are in place for North Canadian, Deer Creek, Chisholm Creek, and South Canadian. Jacobs will manage operations in full compliance with each plan and support renewals, updates, and new site permitting as needed with OCWUT approval.

<!-- ¶9 -->
###### BIOSOLIDS EXPERTISE THAT GOES BEYOND DAY-TO-DAY OPERATIONS

<!-- ¶10 -->
###### TODD WILLIAMS, PE, BCEE, WEF FELLOW _BIOSOLIDS ENGINEERING SME_

<!-- ¶11 -->
Todd brings 45 years of experience in biosolids and residuals management, holding two U.S. patents for biosolids treatment processes and having supported more than 100 biosolids management projects nationwide. Todd will advise the team on dewatering optimization, beneficial reuse alternatives, and longterm program development, including the drying capacity evaluation at North Canadian.

<!-- ¶12 -->
###### DREW MEARNS **|** _O&M BIOSOLIDS SME_

<!-- ¶13 -->
Drew brings 34 years of hands-on biosolids experience spanning the full lifecycle from dewatering and storage through land application, disposal, and emerging beneficial reuse alternatives. Drew advises Jacobs’ O&M projects nationally on cost-effective biosolids best management practices and is deeply familiar with national residuals management firms, their capabilities, and their pricing, giving OCWUT direct access to the market knowledge needed to build a competitive and resilient program. 189_009385

<!-- ¶14 -->
###### _Each facility presents its own operational profile._

<!-- ¶15 -->
**North Canadian** serves as the primary solids processing hub, handling thickening, dewatering, lime stabilization, and stacking pad storage for the system. Site observations confirmed elevated hydrogen sulfide conditions in solids handling areas and a significant presence of plastics in the lime-stabilized biosolids, a nuisance for landowners and a compliance risk for the land application program. Jacobs will begin by restoring debris removal equipment to eliminate foreign material from biosolids before land application. If existing equipment can't achieve the required performance, we'll evaluate additional solutions and present recommendations to OCWUT before implementation. We’ll evaluate covered storage options at the underutilized 2.5-acre compost pad that would further reduce biosolids volume, expand drying capacity, and control odors. This would produce a better-suited product for land application.

<!-- ¶16 -->
**Deer Creek** sits adjacent to a residential development, making odor during sludge handling a high-visibility community concern. The RFP requires hydrogen peroxide dosed at 300 mg/L in the sludge transfer line prior to the belt press, a protocol that should deliver an 86% reduction in hydrogen sulfide and a 93% reduction in mercaptans. Site observations indicate this dosing hasn't been consistently implemented. Jacobs will have this protocol in place from day one, coordinated with ferrous chloride feed adjustments to stabilize belt press room conditions and reduce community impact. We'll also fully support the solids processing upgrades coming online at Deer Creek, proactively optimizing the system as improvements are commissioned.

<!-- ¶17 -->
OCWUT-16-26 | O&M OF WASTEWATER FACILITIES AND SOLIDS MANAGEMENT PROGRAM SERVICES  | **127**

## Page 137

<!-- ¶1 -->
**Chisholm Creek** transfers liquid sludge to North Canadian for downstream processing, a dependency that requires active coordination to avoid hauling inefficiencies and storage pressure at the receiving facility. Jacobs will manage liquid hauling to minimize truck traffic and the safety risks that come with frequent tractor-trailer operations at the facility. We'll also conduct a detailed review of the transfer process to identify opportunities to reduce load volume through thickening improvements or mobile dewatering, directly reducing both transportation costs and traffic exposure.

<!-- ¶2 -->
**South Canadian** is undergoing a complete facility transformation, with a new 8.66MGD activated sludge plant anticipated to come online in July 2026, concurrent with Jacobs' operational start date. Rather than inheriting an aging system mid-construction, we’ll take over a newly commissioned facility. Our transition planning specifically accounts for a seamless handoff of new dewatering, aerobic digestion, and disk thickening equipment, with operator training, startup support, and process documentation in place before we assume operations. From day one, our biosolids experts, including Todd Williams and Drew Mearns, will establish baseline performance, optimize polymer dosing and dewatering protocols on the new equipment, and build a process control foundation that maximizes cake solids quality and land application efficiency. We will walk in prepared.

<!-- ¶3 -->
**Hefner Water Treatment Plant** lime residuals represent a distinct program within scope. Jacobs will manage collection and transport of lime residuals to approved recipients or City-designated locations in accordance with the Hefner Water

<!-- ¶4 -->
Treatment Plant Lime Residuals Plan and applicable regulations, applying the same operational discipline used across the wastewater biosolids program.

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### **Sludge Generation, Processing, and Class B Treatment**

<!-- ¶6 -->
OCWUT's sludge program operates under four ODEQ-approved Sludge Management Plans, 40 CFR Part 503 Class B requirements, and Schedule 14 and 21 obligations that carry direct financial consequences when storage thresholds are exceeded or reporting lapses **(Exhibit 5-8)** . With four facilities, around 170 permitted land application sites, and roughly 13,500 acres under management, the compliance exposure is continuous, and the only way to stay ahead of it is to manage solids as one fully integrated system. Jacobs will connect every stage from generation through final disposal under a single coordinated program, where dewatering performance, storage levels, hauling schedules, and land application decisions all inform each other. That coordination protects OCWUT's compliance record and drives direct cost savings through optimized polymer use, reduced hauling volume, and the elimination of emergency response premiums.

<!-- ¶7 -->
###### **Storage Management and Backlog Prevention**

<!-- ¶8 -->
Storage management is where Schedule 14 and Schedule 21 requirements converge into direct financial and operational risk. Schedule 14 limits storage to 3 days of production without authorization, and Schedule 21 triggers action and potential fees when storage exceeds defined thresholds. Jacobs will manage well below these limits, not to them. Our structured Storage Management Protocol

<!-- ¶14 -->
###### **Odor Management in Sludge Operations**

<!-- ¶15 -->
Sludge handling is one of the highest contributors to odor risk across the OCWUT system, and Schedule 14 requires sludge and biosolids management to minimize offensive odors at facilities, along transport routes, and at land application sites. We describe detailed odor control strategies in the Odor Control Plan. Here, we focus on how sludge management decisions directly influence odor outcomes, as sludge management decisions and odor outcomes are inseparable. **We manage them as one system.**

<!-- ¶16 -->
###### **Transport and Hauling Controls**

<!-- ¶17 -->
Jacobs will transport sludge and biosolids safely, cleanly, and in full compliance with Schedule 14 requirements. All loads will be properly contained, hauling equipment will be maintained to prevent leaks or spills, routes will be selected and managed to minimize community impact, and drivers will follow defined procedures for safe transport and documentation. Where subcontract haulers are used, Jacobs will maintain full oversight and accountability for performance.

<!-- ¶18 -->
**We’ll conduct transport in a manner that eliminates spill potential with immediate cleanup and required notifications in the event of any release, consistent with Schedule 14 requirements.**

<!-- ¶19 -->
###### **Land Application Program Management**

<!-- ¶20 -->
Managing OCWUT's land application program across approximately 170 sites and 13,500 acres requires daily coordination and disciplined execution. Jacobs will schedule and rotate sites based on suitability, weather conditions, soil conditions, hauling logistics, and storage levels. We’ll maintain all required records and coordinate closely with landowners to support reliable operations.

<!-- ¶21 -->
We will work with OCWUT to develop and maintain a Sensitive Sites list, providing prior notification before any biosolids application at identified locations and applying additional controls to reduce community and regulatory risk. When conditions are not favorable at any site, we’ll adjust or delay operations rather than create risk. When additional capacity is needed, we will support identification and permitting of new sites with OCWUT approval.

<!-- ¶22 -->
###### **Testing, Monitoring, and Compliance Reporting**

<!-- ¶23 -->
Jacobs will complete all required sludge and biosolids testing and reporting in accordance with permits and Schedule 14 requirements. We’ll collect samples at required frequencies, manage them under chain-of-custody procedures, and analyze them using approved methods. We’ll review data for compliance before submission. We will maintain organized, accessible records to support audits, inspections, and regulatory review and will use this data to identify trends and adjust operations before issues develop **.**

<!-- ¶24 -->
###### **External Communications Protocol**

<!-- ¶25 -->
All external communications related to sludge and biosolids management will be directed through OCWUT's Public Information staff, as required by Schedule 14. Jacobs will train all staff and subcontractors on this requirement and establish clear internal escalation procedures. We will document all inquiries and complaints and track them to resolution.

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **Contingency Planning and Upset Response**

<!-- ¶2 -->
Jacobs will maintain contingency plans for the conditions most likely to create operational pressure, including equipment outages, dewatering upsets, weather-related restrictions, hauling disruptions, and site unavailability. Wet-weather events represent one of the most predictable contingency scenarios for OCWUT's program. Extended rain periods limit land application availability while sludge generation continues, creating storage pressure. We will maintain pre-identified contingency disposal options, including landfill arrangements consistent with Exhibit B, so that wet-weather restrictions never result in storage threshold violations or fee exposure. Each contingency plan will define clear triggers, response actions, and communication steps so that response is immediate rather than improvised.

<!-- ¶3 -->
###### **Performance Tracking and Continuous Improvement**

<!-- ¶4 -->
Jacobs will track key performance indicators (KPIs) including pad utilization, tons processed and hauled, odor incidents, hauling performance, compliance metrics, and site utilization. Corrective actions will be implemented when trends indicate risk, and monthly reporting will provide OCWUT with clear visibility into program performance.

<!-- ¶5 -->
**This approach maintains compliance with COM-3 and COM-5, protects OCWUT's budget from fee exposure, and builds a sludge management program that becomes more reliable, efficient, and resilient over time.**

###### **OPERATIONAL INTEGRATION PLAN**

OCWUT’s planned capital improvements represent a significant investment in system capacity, reliability, safety, and modernization. These projects will reshape how your facilities handle disinfection, biosolids processing, and solids disposal—and each one needs to transition smoothly from construction to daily operations without disrupting treatment performance or permit compliance. That’s what this Operational Integration Plan is designed to deliver.

<!-- ¶9 -->
Jacobs will provide a structured, repeatable process for bringing each new facility or upgrade into full operational status with the staffing, training, maintenance systems, and risk management in place before day one of operations. **We’ve built this framework around the specific CIP projects, and it will adapt as OCWUT’s capital program evolves (Exhibit 5-10)** .
