## Page 140

<!-- ¶1 -->
OCWUT also retains the right to modify, defer, or add CIP projects during the contract term. This plan establishes the integration framework; we will develop project-specific integration plans as each CIP project approaches commissioning, detailing the staffing, training, maintenance, and risk management steps tailored to that project’s scope and timeline.

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### **Staffing, Resource, and Cost Impacts**

<!-- ¶3 -->
Each planned CIP project will change how OCWUT’s facilities are staffed, maintained, and operated. We’ll identify and plan for these changes well before each project reaches commissioning, working through the Schedule 16 design phases to build a complete picture of staffing, resource, and cost impacts. The following analysis addresses each project’s anticipated impacts. For detailed staffing levels and organizational structure, refer to our **Staffing and Training Plan earlier in this section.**

<!-- ¶4 -->
###### _Staffing Levels and Classifications_

<!-- ¶5 -->
•<sup>**OSHG (Deer Creek and North Canadian):**The transition from gaseous chlorine</sup> to OSHG will fundamentally change chemical handling at both facilities. Current operations involve 150-lb cylinder handling at Deer Creek and one-ton cylinders at North Canadian—each with its own safety protocols, PSM/RMP compliance requirements, and operator skill sets. On-site generation shifts the operation to bulk liquid chemical management with electrolytic cell systems, brine handling, and hydrogen gas ventilation. We anticipate that existing operations staff can absorb hypochlorite generation responsibilities with targeted training, rather than requiring additional staff. However, the maintenance skill set will need to expand to include electrolytic cell systems, brine quality management, and chemical feed calibration. We’ll finalize staffing projections as system designs are completed.

<!-- ¶6 -->
•<sup>**Biosolids Heat Drying Facility (Deer Creek):**This is an entirely new process unit</sup> requiring dedicated operator coverage. Heat drying demands 24/7 operation with specialized skills in thermal system management, emissions monitoring, material handling, and high-temperature safety. We’ll work with OCWUT’s design engineers through the Schedule 16 phases to determine the specific staffing model, including shift structure, operator classifications, and whether dedicated maintenance technicians are needed on-site or can be supported by our Regional Maintenance Team during the initial operating period. Our operations and design subject matter experts (SMEs) will support OCWUT to ensure safety, sizing, and equipment reliability is captured during the design phases of the Deer Creek expansion project.

<!-- ¶7 -->
•<sup>**Land Application to Landfill Transition (Systemwide):**The shift from land</sup> application to landfill disposal will reduce certain staffing requirements while creating new ones. Current land application operations require significant lab resources such as daily pH and fecal coliform testing on each day’s biosolids production and the field staff coordinating application logistics. Moving to landfill disposal will reduce lab testing requirements and associated staffing, potentially freeing approximately one FTE on the solids side currently dedicated to lime stabilization activities. In exchange, hauling coordination will increase, and we’ll work with subcontractors to manage transport logistics to area landfills. We will work with OCWUT to confirm this approach remains the best once landfill disposal is finalized.

<!-- ¶8 -->
###### _Maintenance Staffing and Specialized Skill Sets_

<!-- ¶9 -->
Each CIP project introduces equipment and systems that require new maintenance competencies. **We’ll identify these skill gaps early and address them through a combination of on-site training, manufacturer certification, and support from our national technical resources.**

<!-- ¶10 -->
•<sup>**For OSHG systems**, maintenance staff will need proficiency in electrolytic cell</sup> maintenance and replacement cycles, brine system management and softening system upkeep, sodium hypochlorite storage tank inspection, and chemical feed system calibration. At South Canadian, where PSI/Microclor® onsite generation equipment is already being installed as part of the current construction, we will gain direct operating experience with this equipment beginning at contract start, building institutional knowledge that transfers directly to the Deer Creek and North Canadian installations.

<!-- ¶11 -->
•<sup>**For the heat drying facility**, maintenance requirements will include thermal</sup> equipment and dryer system maintenance, emissions control systems, conveyance and material handling equipment, and high-temperature safety system inspection. Our Regional Maintenance Team and national technical resources, including biosolids SMEs with direct thermal drying experience, will supplement on-site staff for specialized maintenance needs during the initial integration period.

<!-- ¶12 -->
###### _Use of Subcontractors or Third-Party Services_

<!-- ¶13 -->
Certain specialized services associated with the new facilities will require subcontractor support. For hypochlorite generation, this may include electrolytic cell manufacturer service agreements for major overhauls and warranty-covered repairs, particularly important given that PSI repair parts are proprietary and cartridge replacements cost approximately $20,000 each. For the heat drying facility, specialized services will likely include emissions testing, thermal system

<!-- ¶14 -->
OCWUT-16-26 | O&M OF WASTEWATER FACILITIES AND SOLIDS MANAGEMENT PROGRAM SERVICES  | **131**

## Page 141

<!-- ¶1 -->
inspections, and potentially dryer manufacturer service support during the initial operating period. **All subcontractors will be integrated into the work management system (NexGen EAM) with scheduled activities, documentation requirements, and performance tracking.**

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### _Emergency Response and On-Call Coverage_

<!-- ¶3 -->
**We’ll update emergency response and on-call coverage as new facilities come online.** For hypochlorite generation, this includes chemical release response protocols, system failure contingencies (with reversion to delivered chemical supply), and hydrogen gas safety monitoring. For heat drying, coverage addresses thermal emergency response, emissions exceedance protocols, and fire/explosion prevention. On-call rotations will expand to cover new process areas, with all emergency response procedures developed during the Schedule 16 Startup Services phase.

<!-- ¶4 -->
###### _Expected Impacts on Operating and Maintenance Costs_

<!-- ¶5 -->
Each CIP project will affect operating and maintenance costs across multiple categories, including labor, utilities, chemicals, maintenance materials, and other recurring expenses. Because these projects are still in design or pre-design stages, specific cost impacts can’t be quantified today. However, we recognize the directional nature of these changes:

<!-- ¶6 -->
- <sup>**OSHG**will shift chemical costs from purchased product to raw materials (salt/</sup> brine) and electrical demand, while eliminating PSM/RMP compliance costs at both Deer Creek and North Canadian, a meaningful reduction in both compliance burden and safety risk.

<!-- ¶7 -->
- <sup>**The heat drying facility**will introduce new utility costs (significant energy</sup> demand for thermal processing), emissions monitoring expenses, and consumable costs, along with the dedicated staffing discussed above.

<!-- ¶8 -->
- <sup>**The landfill transition**will replace land application labor and equipment</sup> costs with hauling and tipping fees. Our analysis suggests these costs may be comparable, with landfill potentially offering cost advantages in the near term, given the investment otherwise needed to bring odor control and biosolids quality to compliant levels for continued land application.

<!-- ¶9 -->
As each project progresses through the Schedule 16 design phases, we’ll work with OCWUT to identify, quantify, and formalize cost adjustments through the Service Approval Form and Amendment process. After we have operated a complete CIP project for 2 years, OCWUT will conduct an audit using GAAP principles to determine fair and equitable cost adjustments. We’ll maintain detailed cost tracking in NexGen from the first day of operations for each new facility, providing OCWUT with transparent, auditable data to support that process.

<!-- ¶10 -->
###### _Hypochlorite Transport Plan_

<!-- ¶11 -->
Schedule 18 requires a plan to transport hypochlorite generated at the Deer Creek facility to the Chisholm Creek facility. We will develop a detailed Hypochlorite Transport Plan in coordination with OCWUT as the Deer Creek generation system design progresses. Because the system isn’t estimated for completion until 2029, a fully specified logistics plan would be premature at this stage. However, the planning framework will address:

<!-- ¶12 -->
- <sup>**Logistics and cost-effectiveness:**transport method, vehicle requirements,</sup> frequency, and route considerations weighed against the alternative of direct chemical delivery to Chisholm Creek. Our initial assessment, informed by our team’s operational analysis, is that hauling costs between facilities may be comparable to direct delivery, making the cost-benefit analysis a key decision point for OCWUT.

<!-- ¶13 -->
- <sup>**Safety and compliance:**chemical handling and transport protocols, DOT</sup> compliance with sodium hypochlorite transport, and spill response procedures.

<!-- ¶14 -->
- <sup>**Responsibility and delivery model:**whether transport is handled by a chemical</sup> transport subcontractor, the hypochlorite system vendor, or through another arrangement. Jacobs does not anticipate self-performing bulk chemical transport between facilities.

<!-- ¶15 -->
- <sup>**Contingency:**temporary reversion to delivered chemical supply if transport is</sup> interrupted or the generation system is offline.

<!-- ¶16 -->
###### _Biosolids Transition—Land Application to Landfill Disposal_

<!-- ¶17 -->
The systemwide transition from land application to landfill disposal is one of the most significant operational shifts in this contract. It affects all facilities, changes subcontractor relationships, alters lab and monitoring requirements, and sets the stage for the eventual integration of the Deer Creek heat drying facility. We’ll develop a detailed Biosolids Transition Plan in coordination with OCWUT as the timeline takes shape.

<!-- ¶18 -->
OCWUT-16-26 | O&M OF WASTEWATER FACILITIES AND SOLIDS MANAGEMENT PROGRAM SERVICES  | **132**

## Page 142

<!-- ¶1 -->
A key advantage of initiating the landfill transition early in the contract, rather than waiting for the heat drying facility, is that it simplifies the eventual thermal drying integration. Instead of changing multiple elements simultaneously (landfill contracts, hauling logistics, subcontractor scope, regulatory approvals), the early transition establishes those relationships and logistics so that when the thermal dryer comes online, it’s a single internal change rather than a multi-party coordination challenge. The planning framework will address:

<!-- ¶2 -->
- <sup>**Phasing:**how and when each facility transitions, coordinated with OCWUT’s</sup> priorities and the Deer Creek heat drying facility timeline. We may recommend a selective early transition, for example, diverting biosolids with quality concerns (such as paper debris) to landfill first while maintaining land application for compliant material during the transition period.

<!-- ¶3 -->
- <sup>**Hauling logistics:**transport distances, vehicle and equipment requirements,</sup> landfill agreements, and scheduling. Area landfills have indicated capacity for OKC’s biosolids volume, though we will verify capacity commitments and understand restrictions (moisture content limits, percentage of total landfill capacity for wet material) before finalizing agreements.

<!-- ¶4 -->
- <sup>**Cost of transport and final disposal:**framework for estimating per-wet-ton</sup> and per-dry-ton costs, tipping fees, and fuel. The transition will replace current subcontractor costs (currently managed through Synagro) with direct hauling and disposal costs.

<!-- ¶5 -->
- <sup>**Staffing and lab adjustments:**reduced land application crew requirements,</sup> re-deployment of lab personnel currently performing daily biosolids testing, and changes to monitoring protocols once land application ceases.

<!-- ¶6 -->
- <sup>**Regulatory transition:**ODEQ notification requirements, permit modifications,</sup> and closure of land application sites.

<!-- ¶7 -->
- <sup>**Contingency:**continued land application capability if the heat drying facility</sup> timeline shifts or landfill capacity issues arise.

<!-- ¶8 -->
- For our biosolids management strategy, including current operations and long-term solids handling, refer to our Solids Management Plan in this section.

<!-- ¶9 -->
###### **Training and Knowledge Transfer**

<!-- ¶10 -->
**New systems and technologies demand new skills and we’ll build those skills before operational handover, not after.** For each CIP project, our training approach follows a structured sequence:

<!-- ¶11 -->
- <sup>**Manufacturer and vendor training:**Wewill coordinate with equipment</sup> manufacturers for both factory and on-site training during commissioning. For hypochlorite generation, this means hands-on training with PSI/Microclor® electrolytic cell systems, brine management, and control interfaces, leveraging

<!-- ¶12 -->
our direct experience operating PSI units at similar facilities. For heat drying, manufacturer training will cover thermal system operation, emissions monitoring, material handling, and emergency procedures.

<!-- ¶13 -->
- <sup>**Construction contractor and integrator coordination:**Jacobs staff will</sup> participate in startup and proofing activities, gaining hands-on familiarity with each system in its installed configuration before assuming full responsibility.

<!-- ¶14 -->
- <sup>**Process operations training:**Coordination with OCWUT’s engineers to</sup> understand new treatment configurations, control strategies, and performance expectations for each upgraded process.

<!-- ¶15 -->
•<sup>**Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) and Standard Maintenance**</sup> **Procedures (SMPs) development:** We’ll develop SOPs and SMPs for each new facility or process using construction contract-provided O&M information and engineer-provided process operations information, as required by Schedule 16 Startup Services.

<!-- ¶16 -->
Training will begin well before operational handover, with staff participating in commissioning, proofing, and startup activities to build hands-on familiarity. For our comprehensive training program structure, refer to our **Staffing and Training Plan earlier in this section.**

<!-- ¶17 -->
###### **Maintenance Program Adjustments**

<!-- ¶18 -->
Every new asset needs to be absorbed into the maintenance program from day one. Our approach to maintenance integration connects directly to our overall Maintenance Plan and leverages NexGen EAM as the single system of record.

<!-- ¶19 -->
•<sup>**CMMS Configuration and Asset Additions.**For each completed CIP project,</sup> we will configure NexGen EAM to incorporate new assets with complete documentation: manufacturer data, model and serial numbers, install dates, warranty terms, and criticality rankings. We’ll establish parent-child asset relationships linked to facility process areas, build Bills of Material from manufacturer documentation for efficient parts identification, and configure automated reporting so new assets are captured in KPI tracking and monthly reports from day one.

<!-- ¶20 -->
•<sup>**Spare Parts and Consumables.**We’ll develop spare parts inventory strategies for</sup> each new facility before operational handover, identifying critical spares based on manufacturer recommendations and our experience, establishing minimum/ maximum inventory levels in NexGen, and coordinating initial stocking with commissioning timelines. For hypochlorite generation, this includes proprietary electrolytic cell cartridges and brine system components. For heat drying, it includes thermal system consumables and emissions monitoring supplies. New consumables such as salt/brine for generation, fuel for drying, and polymer changes will be integrated into procurement schedules.

<!-- ¶21 -->
OCWUT-16-26 | O&M OF WASTEWATER FACILITIES AND SOLIDS MANAGEMENT PROGRAM SERVICES  | **133**

<!-- ¶1 -->
•<sup>**Warranty Tracking and Compliance.**New CIP equipment carries manufacturer warranties that represent significant value to OCWUT. We’ll enter all warranty terms,</sup> conditions, and expiration dates into NexGen during commissioning and flag warranty-covered assets in the work order system, so corrective maintenance triggers a warranty review before any repair work proceeds. We’ll build warranty-required maintenance tasks into the preventive maintenance (PM) schedule and manage defective work claims through the 2-year maintenance bond per Schedule 16. Updated warranty lists will be included in monthly reports.

<!-- ¶2 -->
- <sup>**Long-Term Lifecycle Maintenance Planning.**We will update the Asset Management Plan after each CIP project, incorporating new assets into lifecycle cost projections</sup> and our Asset Replacement Modeling (ARM) tool. We’ll adjust risk profiles and criticality rankings, develop optimized PM/predictive maintenance (PdM) strategies starting with manufacturer recommendations and refining with NexGen operating data, and feed updated projections into the annual Facilities Status Report and CIP recommendations due each October 15.

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **Experience with OSHG**

<!-- ¶2 -->
The planned transition to OSHG at multiple OCWUT facilities is one of the most operationally significant changes in this contract. It eliminates gaseous chlorine—and with it, the PSM/RMP compliance requirements at North Canadian and Deer Creek—while introducing a fundamentally different chemical management approach. Jacobs brings direct, relevant experience to this transition. **See Section 3 – Experience and Qualifications of the Firm and Management Team** for **detailed discussion of our OSHG O&M experience.**

<!-- ¶3 -->
Regardless of the specific systems OCWUT ultimately selects for Deer Creek and North Canadian, the core operational and maintenance considerations are consistent across OSHG technology. Our experience gives us direct knowledge of:

<!-- ¶4 -->
- <sup>**Electrolytic cell maintenance and replacement cycles**,including the proprietary cartridge systems (such as PSI/Microclor® units, where cartridges cost approximately</sup> $20,000 each) that require careful lifecycle management and warranty tracking.

<!-- ¶5 -->
- <sup>**Brine quality management and softening system upkeep**—critical for protecting cell life and maintaining consistent hypochlorite production.</sup>

<!-- ¶6 -->
- <sup>**Sodium hypochlorite storage and degradation management**,including strength monitoring, turnover rate optimization, and temperature control to maintain effective</sup> chlorine concentration.

<!-- ¶7 -->
- <sup>**Hydrogen gas ventilation and safety monitoring**—a key safety consideration for on-site generation systems that require dedicated monitoring and response protocols.</sup>

<!-- ¶8 -->
- <sup>**Integration with existing disinfection control systems and SCADA**—transitioning from gas feed control to liquid chemical dosing with automated residual feedback.</sup>

<!-- ¶9 -->
- <sup>**PSM/RMP transition**—asignificant benefit of the conversion is the elimination of gaseous chlorine PSM requirements at both North Canadian and Deer Creek. Currently,</sup> both facilities maintain PSM/RMP programs for their chlorine gas systems. Once OSHG replaces gas, those requirements and their associated compliance burden, training requirements, and safety risk are eliminated. This is a meaningful improvement in both worker safety and regulatory simplification for OCWUT.

<!-- ¶10 -->
We will leverage the direct operating experience we’ll build at South Canadian’s new PSI/Microclor® units (online by contract start) to inform our integration planning for the Deer Creek and North Canadian installations. This gives us facility-specific knowledge of the same equipment family before the next generation systems are commissioned.

<!-- ¶1 -->
We’ll offer employment to qualified and interested incumbent staff and work to retain the experience, plant knowledge, and site familiarity they bring to the program. Our goal is not simply to replace one workforce with another, but to carry forward the strengths of the existing team while integrating our operating practices, tools, training systems, and support resources to strengthen performance across the program.

<!-- ¶2 -->
We also understand that transition creates uncertainty for employees and their families. We address that directly through clear communication, visible leadership, and a structured onboarding process that sets expectations early. During transition, we’ll host informational sessions to introduce Jacobs, explain how we operate, review benefits and roles, and give employees a clear understanding of next steps. Our experienced HR and employee relations professionals will support this process, and employees will have direct access to regional and project leadership throughout.

<!-- ¶3 -->
That approach has measurable results. As shown in **Exhibit 5-14** , **employees who join Jacobs through transition consistently report stronger engagement and higher satisfaction than before the change** . That response reflects our

<!-- ¶4 -->
**Exhibit 5-14.** _Staff Satisfaction Improves Significantly After Jacobs Takes Over Contract Operations_

<!-- ¶5 -->
commitment to investing in people, communicating clearly, supporting professional development, and creating a culture where staff feel supported and see a path for success. When employees experience a well-managed transition, they stay engaged, perform confidently, and contribute to a stable operating environment for the client.

<!-- ¶6 -->
Jacobs will provide accepted employment offers for all management positions prior to execution of the service agreement. Our proposed management team is identified in this proposal, and offers will be extended and accepted before execution, giving OCWUT confidence that the leadership team is committed and in place before transition begins. This is not a post-award action item. **It is a pre-execution commitment.**

<!-- ¶7 -->
We’ll recruit, onboard, train, and schedule the additional personnel needed to satisfy the staffing plan and support continuous operations by the operational start date. We’ll sequence staffing actions to support knowledge transfer, systems access, training, and transfer-of-service activities so personnel are in place early enough to contribute to readiness, not simply arrive at startup.

<!-- ¶8 -->
Workforce readiness will include verification of certifications, background checks, and facility access requirements and implementation of the required training plan. We’ll incorporate safety training, job-specific training, systems training, and crosstraining to confirm adequate and competent coverage across functions. We’ll also establish the required on-call structure to ensure the facilities are continuously supported by trained and qualified personnel.

<!-- ¶9 -->
If staffing gaps occur during transition, we’ll fill them with a combination of preidentified local candidates and experienced regional resources, many of whom hold ODEQ certifications, to maintain required coverage and protect operational readiness while continuing to build a stable long-term team.

<!-- ¶10 -->
###### **Transition Leadership and Coordination**

<!-- ¶11 -->
**We’ll deploy an experienced transition leadership team responsible for planning, coordination, and execution of all transition activities.** This team will serve as the single point of accountability for OCWUT and will maintain alignment across staffing, safety, compliance, asset management, systems integration, and communications. Their role is both strategic and hands-on, with a consistent presence throughout transition.

<!-- ¶12 -->
**Kelly Irving** will serve as Transition Manager, leveraging decades of leadership experience and a strong track record of delivering stable, compliant startups. **Mike Boven** will support execution in the field, focusing on day-to-day

###### **OCWUT-Specific Operational Readiness**

<!-- ¶8 -->
Jacobs will tailor transition activities to the scope and operating profile of OCWUT's wastewater facilities, applying a wastewater-focused startup approach rather than a one-size-fits-all transition model. Early efforts will focus on understanding and validating the systems most critical to reliable startup: biological treatment processes, biosolids handling and dewatering, lime stabilization, chemical feed systems, electrical infrastructure, plant control systems, laboratory operations, certification

<!-- ¶9 -->

<!-- ¶10 -->
South Canadian presents a distinct transition challenge and opportunity. With the new 8.6-MGD activated sludge facility anticipated to come online in July 2026, we’ll take over a newly commissioned plant mid-transition. Our transition planning accounts for coordination with the construction contractor on equipment commissioning and warranty management, new equipment familiarization for operations staff, and startup protocol development for processes that will have limited operating history by January 1, 2027. This is not an unfamiliar scenario

<!-- ¶11 -->
requirements, and QA/QC procedures. We’ll also prioritize the collection system interface at Witcher Pump Station, the biosolids land application program, and the elevated H2S conditions documented at North Canadian and Deer Creek, which will require site-specific safety protocols and equipment readiness verification during transition.

for our team. That experience is built into how we’ll approach South Canadian from day one.

To manage all of this in a disciplined way, **we’ll use our comprehensive transition checklist workbook, which includes 1,730 action items across 30 workstreams, combining OCWUT-specific startup tasks with our internal controls, reviews, and readiness verification.** Organized by workstream, the checklist allows SMEs and transition leaders to track responsibilities, dependencies, due dates, and readiness status at a detailed level, serving as a standing reference in every transition team meeting from award through startup.

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### **Communication and Knowledge Transfer**

<!-- ¶6 -->
We’ll implement a transition communications plan that defines roles, responsibilities, communication pathways, and meeting cadence and regular coordination meetings with OCWUT to review progress, resolve issues, and confirm readiness. Knowledge transfer will follow a structured approach combining **documentation review, staff engagement, and direct observation of operations across all shifts,** capturing both formal procedures and the practical, experiencebased knowledge that only comes from working alongside people who know these facilities.

<!-- ¶7 -->
###### **Training and Operational Readiness**

<!-- ¶8 -->
During the transition period, we’ll prepare staff to assume their responsibilities safely, confidently, and effectively on the operational start date. Before performing independent duties, personnel will complete required safety training, job-specific operational training, and training on OCWUT systems, procedures, and work processes in accordance with the training plan.

<!-- ¶9 -->
During transition, our staff will shadow existing personnel and maintain a strong field presence across shifts to develop a detailed understanding of facility operations, maintenance practices, system behavior, and site-specific requirements. **This boots-on-the-ground approach will allow our team to observe operating conditions, workflows, decision points, and coordination practices in real time while respecting that the incumbent remains responsible for performing the work until the operational start date.** This familiarization will be reinforced through cross-training and systems training so personnel are prepared not only for their assigned roles but to provide reliable coverage across all facilities.

<!-- ¶10 -->
Training will continue beyond day one through refreshers, role-specific development, and ongoing reinforcement of safe operating practices, compliance requirements, and standard procedures, which will build confidence, promote consistency, and support reliable performance.

<!-- ¶11 -->
###### **Asset Validation and Capital Planning**

<!-- ¶12 -->
During the transition period, we’ll work with OCWUT to validate the baseline physical condition of the facilities as required under Schedule 19. This will include review and field verification of process equipment, structures, electrical systems, instrumentation, controls, and other critical assets so that facility condition is clearly documented and mutually understood before startup. Our team will use this process to identify discrepancies, confirm asset conditions, and support development of a defensible baseline for future accountability and asset management. We’ll coordinate the baseline physical condition of facilities report with OCWUT, and it will be jointly signed prior to the operational start date in accordance with transition requirements.

<!-- ¶13 -->
Building directly on the baseline condition assessment findings, we’ll provide prioritized recommendations for capital improvements and reliability enhancements within 90 days of the operational start date. These recommendations will be grounded in documented field conditions, aligned with life-cycle planning, and structured to support OCWUT's annual CIP budget development process consistent with Schedule 20 requirements.

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### **SOLIDS MANAGEMENT PLAN**

<!-- ¶3 -->
Effective solids management is one of the responsibilities in this contract with the highest stakes and one of the most visible. Backlog on the stacking pad creates direct fee exposure. Odor during dewatering drives community complaints and can limit access to operations and maintenance. Belt press downtime creates cascading risk across storage, hauling, land application, and compliance. OCWUT does not need a solids program that reacts to problems. You need one that prevents them.

<!-- ¶4 -->
**Jacobs will deliver a fully integrated Solids Management Plan with clear operational triggers, daily monitoring, and structured escalation protocols designed to keep OCWUT ahead of Schedule 21 thresholds and avoid deductions under both COM-3 and COM-5.**

<!-- ¶5 -->
We’ll deploy an experienced team to support, plan, monitor, and evaluate the current solids program, bringing together expertise from compliance, engineering, delivery services, and on-site management. We’ll coordinate with a subcontractor to manage biosolids across all four facilities as a single, connected system. This plan aligns directly with our Odor Control Plan and Process Control Plan so that solids, process performance, and odor are managed together rather than in isolation. For land application, we'll prioritize agricultural sites outside of densely populated areas and plan truck routes that keep biosolids transport off residential and commercial corridors. This approach directly reduces odor exposure and truck traffic in the neighborhoods that matter most to Oklahoma City residents turning a common community concern into a non-issue.

<!-- ¶6 -->

<!-- ¶7 -->
###### **Understanding of OCWUT Solids Management Requirements**

<!-- ¶8 -->
Managing solids across a four-facility system of this scale requires systemwide coordination and a clear understanding of how sludge moves through the system from generation to final disposal. We have reviewed the Schedule 14 Sludge Management Plans, the Schedule 21 Solids Management requirements, and the broader Service Agreement scope. We understand the operational constraints and performance expectations that will drive success at OCWUT.

<!-- ¶9 -->
**North Canadian** serves as the primary solids processing and storage hub, with thickening, dewatering, lime stabilization, and stacking pad storage. Site observations confirmed elevated hydrogen sulfide levels in solids handling areas affecting safety, access, and reliability. We’ll address these conditions immediately through coordinated process control, odor management, and maintenance planning.

<!-- ¶10 -->
**Deer Creek** presents elevated hydrogen sulfide levels and inconsistent cake quality that directly affect odor and hauling performance. We’ll continue hydrogen peroxide dosing, evaluate dosing location, and assess additional solutions to stabilize belt press room conditions.

<!-- ¶11 -->
**Chisholm Creek** relies on coordinated transfer of liquid sludge to North Canadian for downstream processing. This dependency requires proactive planning to optimize solids concentration being transferred, reduce hauling volume, and avoid unnecessary cost and storage pressure at the receiving facility.

<!-- ¶1 -->
We’ll continually evaluate opportunities to improve solids processing, focusing on practical improvements that increase reliability, reduce cost, and strengthen compliance based on actual operating conditions at OCWUT.

<!-- ¶2 -->
Schedule 14 and Schedule 21 make solids management a real-time operational responsibility. Biosolids storage is limited to 3 days of production without authorization, and performance thresholds are tied directly to financial outcomes.

<!-- ¶3 -->
###### **Integrated Solids Management Strategy**

<!-- ¶4 -->
###### **Biosolids Processing and Dewatering Operations**

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### **Jacobs will manage OCWUT’s solids program as one coordinated system from generation through final disposal.**

<!-- ¶6 -->
**Reliable dewatering performance is the foundation of the solids program.** When dewatering performs consistently, storage remains controlled, hauling stays on schedule, and land application remains compliant. When it does not, issues escalate quickly across the system.

<!-- ¶7 -->
Our approach begins with understanding how solids are produced, how they move between facilities, and how they are processed and managed in real time. We’ll balance solids production and transfer across facilities so that no single site becomes a bottleneck. Hauling schedules will be aligned with daily production and storage conditions rather than fixed assumptions.

<!-- ¶8 -->
We’ll monitor key process parameters daily, including sludge blanket levels, holding tank levels, belt press performance, cake solids, polymer usage, lime usage, and tons processed. These metrics will be tracked through daily operating logs and reviewed by our supervisors each day.

<!-- ¶9 -->
We’ll integrate solids management with the biosolids land application program, across approximately 170 permitted sites covering about 13,500 acres, requiring daily coordination of hauling, site selection, regulatory documentation, and communication with stakeholders. We’ll account for facility-specific logistics, including transport times and construction constraints at South Canadian. Contingency protocols for equipment downtime and site unavailability will be established in advance, so the system remains stable under changing conditions.

<!-- ¶10 -->
Our operators will maintain stable feed conditions, optimize polymer dosing to maximize efficiency and reduce cost, and maintain lime addition to meet Class B requirements. Production will be tracked against storage and hauling capacity in real time, so adjustments can be made before conditions deteriorate. Processlevel targets, including minimum cake solids, polymer dosing efficiency, and daily throughput will be established during transition and tracked as leading indicators of storage risk, enabling our team to catch performance drift before it affects pad utilization.

<!-- ¶11 -->
A well-managed solids program also strengthens OCWUT's wet weather resilience. When storage is actively maintained well below thresholds and contingency disposal options are pre-arranged, the system can absorb the pressure of wet weather land application restrictions without creating compliance risk or backlog.

<!-- ¶12 -->
Dewatering performance will be actively managed through daily review, trend tracking, and proactive maintenance.

<!-- ¶13 -->
**This approach replaces reactive decision-making with structured, predictable performance.**

<!-- ¶14 -->
###### **Biosolids Storage and Backlog Management**

<!-- ¶15 -->
**Storage management is a direct compliance and financial risk driver at OCWUT.** Schedule 14 limits storage to 3 days of production without prior written

## Page 158

<!-- ¶1 -->
authorization, and Schedule 21 fees apply when storage exceeds 50 percent of total capacity without prior written authorization of the General Manager or their designee.

<!-- ¶2 -->
Jacobs will implement a structured Storage Management Protocol built on daily monitoring and proactive intervention at our 35 percent internal trigger. Our operators will track pad utilization each day, and our supervisors will review conditions daily. When utilization approaches 35 percent, the Operations Manager will evaluate hauling schedules, dewatering performance, and land application availability, and implement adjustments before risk develops.

<!-- ¶3 -->
If utilization approaches 50 percent, we’ll seek prior written authorization from the General Manager, submit the required Service Approval Form with an action response plan, and implement corrective actions consistent with Schedule 21, protecting OCWUT from fee exposure while maintaining operational transparency. **We will not manage to the limit. We will manage well below it.**

<!-- ¶4 -->
When land application is not feasible, contingency disposal strategies will be activated to maintain system stability and prevent backlog.

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### **Hauling, Land Application, and Disposal Coordination**

<!-- ¶6 -->
**OCWUT’s biosolids program requires continuous coordination to remain compliant and efficient.** We’ll coordinate hauling to maintain storage within target ranges and adjust schedules based on production and site conditions. We’ll maintain all required documentation for land application, including volumes, site locations, and compliance parameters required for Class B biosolids. We’ll manage site selection and application sequencing in coordination with OCWUT, while supporting the identification and development of additional permitted sites when needed.

<!-- ¶7 -->
We fully support OCWUT's plan to integrate landfill disposal of biosolids by the 2029 target date. We’ll monitor legislative activity that may influence this timeline and adjust planning accordingly. We have already begun building relationships with local landfill operators and will leverage our national project experience to establish a reliable and effective program.

<!-- ¶8 -->
**Schedule 14 requires biosolids to be managed to minimize odor at facilities, along transport routes, and at land application sites.** We’ll implement controlled loading practices, covered transport, and strict manifest tracking. Spill prevention and immediate cleanup procedures will be enforced to meet regulatory requirements. **All external communications regarding biosolids will be coordinated through OCWUT's Public Information staff as required by Schedule 14.** We’ll maintain ready-to-deploy contingency disposal options to support operations when land application is not feasible.

<!-- ¶9 -->
###### **Odor and Environmental Controls**

<!-- ¶10 -->
Solids handling is one of the highest odor risks across the OCWUT system, and process control decisions related to sludge age, detention time, and solids handling directly influences odor generation. **We’ll manage solids, process control, and odor as one integrated system.** This Solids Management Plan is fully aligned with the Odor Control Plan and Process Control Plan as required by Schedule 21.

<!-- ¶11 -->
###### **Monitoring, Reporting, and Compliance Assurance**

<!-- ¶12 -->
Solids compliance is achieved through disciplined monitoring, review, and action. We’ll track daily solids production, processing, hauling, storage levels, and dewatering performance at each facility. Our supervisors will review this data daily and initiate corrective actions before conditions create compliance risk.

<!-- ¶13 -->
Monthly reporting will provide clear visibility into solids performance, including production trends, hauling activity, storage conditions, and corrective actions, structured to align with OCWUT's requirements while providing additional transparency.

<!-- ¶14 -->
**This plan is specifically designed to maintain compliance with both COM-3 and COM-5 through proactive management of solids and odor conditions.**

<!-- ¶15 -->
###### **Continuous Improvement and Risk Management**

<!-- ¶16 -->
**We’ll treat OCWUT's solids program as a long-term performance opportunity.** We’ll continuously evaluate dewatering performance, chemical usage, equipment condition, hauling efficiency, and process integration. **Every improvement will be evaluated based on its impact on compliance, cost, and community.**

<!-- ¶17 -->
###### **Related Program Support**

<!-- ¶18 -->
OCWUT’s solids responsibilities extend beyond wastewater facilities to include residuals management, septage receiving, and industrial pretreatment support. Jacobs will execute each of these programs with the same discipline applied to biosolids operations.

<!-- ¶19 -->
At the Hefner Water Treatment Plant, we’ll coordinate the collection and transport of lime residuals to approved recipients or City-designated locations while maintaining all required documentation and compliance tracking.

<!-- ¶20 -->
At the Witcher Pump Station, we’ll operate the Septage Receiving Program in accordance with Schedule 6, including pH testing, manifest verification, load inspection, chain-of-custody documentation, and daily reporting. **This is a frontline compliance function that we’ll manage with discipline every day.**

<!-- ¶21 -->
We’ll also support the Industrial Pretreatment Program by providing qualified staff, coordinating inspections and sampling, and maintaining required documentation in alignment with program requirements.

_Delivering reliable wastewater O&M across multiple facilities simultaneously while navigating active capital programs, complex biosolids management, and evolving regulatory requirements takes more than operational know-how. It takes a partner with a proven track record doing exactly that, at scale, over the long term._ **_That's what Jacobs brings to OCWUT_** _—relevant experience managing some of the largest and most complex treatment facilities, pumping assets, and solids programs, with the operational coordination to keep critical infrastructure running well._

<!-- ¶3 -->
Our **O&M portfolio spans more than 300 projects** across water and wastewater treatment, collection and distribution systems, water quality laboratories, stormwater management, biosolids, and facility services—covering the full breadth of municipal infrastructure. We've built lasting partnerships with major clients like Baltimore, Baton Rouge, Seattle, San Diego, Tucson, and Wilmington, with an **average relationship of 18 years and a 98% contract renewal rate** . Those aren't just numbers. They reflect the trust our clients place in us to protect their assets, maintain permit compliance, and deliver reliable service to the communities they serve. At **Baltimore's Back River WWTP (180 MGD)** , one of the largest outsourced wastewater treatment programs in the country, we stepped in, cleared a massive biosolids backlog, and restored operational performance under an active consent decree. In **Baton Rouge** , we operate two plants with combined peak capacity exceeding **300 MGD** , serving more than 222,000 residents. In **Wilmington, Delaware** , our team runs a **134-MGD** facility alongside a complex renewable energy biosolids program. In **Jackson, Mississippi,** our **multi-facility contract spans 145 MGD** across two plants and is larger in both scope and contract value than OCWUT's. These programs are among the most demanding outsourced O&M contracts in the country, and we've delivered on every one of them.

<!-- ¶4 -->
Over our **44 years serving clients across Oklahoma** , we've built a deep understanding of the regulatory, operational, and infrastructure landscape that shapes successful utility programs in the region, including permitting requirements, ODEQ compliance, and water quality standards.

<!-- ¶5 -->
We have **operated the City of Duncan's wastewater facilities since 1995** , administering its industrial pretreatment program (IPP) under ODEQ oversight. That's 30 years of working within the same state regulatory environment OCWUT

<!-- ¶6 -->
operates every day and the relationships, institutional knowledge, and compliance track record that come with it. Our Oklahoma experience goes beyond operations: **we've worked directly with OCWUT's Utilities Department for more than a decade** , providing subject matter expertise in risk and resilience, human-machine interface (HMI) programming, cybersecurity, and business process improvements. We've **developed training plans for new OCWUT staff across multiple divisions** , meaning we already know your systems, your processes, and your people. In **Bixby** , we hold a **10-year, $8.3M contract to operate and maintain** a 2.8-MGD sequencing batch reactor (SBR) water reclamation facility and 18 lift stations.

That foundation supported reliable, cost-effective long-term operations, and the City trusted us to lead that transition from the old holding pond system to the new BWRF. When the under-design issue emerged, we brought in technical resources from across the company to evaluate the problem, develop engineering solutions and cost estimates, and work with the City on compliance alternatives. Throughout the process, **we maintained transparent reporting and gave the City direct access to OMFS and Engineering leadership for timely coordination and decision making** .

###### PROJECT CHALLENGES AND HOW WE RESPONDED

<!-- ¶3 -->
###### **WHAT MADE THIS PARTNERSHIP WORK:** **<u>UNIQUE CHARACTERISTICS THAT BENEFITED BOTH PARTIES</u>**

<!-- ¶4 -->
**Transition:** We **transitioned City O&M staff to Jacobs** , providing them with upgraded safety equipment, crucial training, and introduction to our culture of performance and accountability and our proven O&M systems and processes. **Kelly Irving led transition efforts** , coordinating workforce continuity, regulatory readiness, operational control, and transition activities associated with turnover from the incumbent provider.

<!-- ¶5 -->
This partnership worked because we combined immediate operational action with direct, transparent communication. Maintenance specialists, program managers, compliance leads, and operations staff mobilized together from the outset, giving JXN Water immediate access to both field response and clear reporting. By documenting conditions clearly and linking findings to corrective action, we helped stabilize service for 150,000 residents.

<!-- ¶6 -->
**Wastewater System Stabilization and Reporting:** At takeover, the facilities and lift stations had deferred maintenance, limited functionality, and incomplete visibility into system condition. We immediately began a condition assessment across the three WWTPs and 99 pump stations, documented inherited deficiencies, restored pumps, and communications. **Our transparent approach gave JXN Water a clear path to reducing bypasses and improving reliability.**

<!-- ¶7 -->
An additional outcome of this partnership has been the cultural shift within the workforce. We have integrated staff from the City and the prior operating team, investing in professional development. The result has been **measurable improvements in employee engagement and skill development underpinning long-term system reliability.**

<!-- ¶8 -->
###### **<u>PROVEN RESULTS</u>**

<!-- ¶9 -->
**Odor Control:** We restored core process control by **returning aeration equipment to reliable operation** , **improving dewatering performance, and implementing routine wet well cleaning and pump station inspections** . We also **resolved isolated odor concerns** related to onsite biosolids storage through **improved hauling and prompt offsite removal** . Severe odor issues did not become a recurring problem across the plants. Any residual odors were brief, associated only with spill-related events, and improved rapidly as lift stations were repaired and restored to standard Jacobs operating parameters.

<!-- ¶10 -->
- Lift station functionality improved from ~30% operational at takeover to **90% fully operational**

<!-- ¶11 -->
- **Reliability restored across a system** where ~70% of lift stations initially had only one pump and/or no communication system

<!-- ¶12 -->
- **Staff integration from City and prior operator** , with improvements in staff engagement and technical capability

<!-- ¶13 -->
- **50+ person team mobilized and operational within days of interim contract execution**

<!-- ¶14 -->
**Maintenance and Reliability Recovery:** We shifted the system from reactive response to structured asset recovery. We used the condition assessment to identify critical failures, prioritize repairs, and **established a strategic improvement plan focused on the assets that most affected compliance and continuity** . We also deployed our CMMS at contract start, using assessment data to create a live, tracked maintenance program from day one.

<!-- ¶15 -->

###### FACILITY OVERVIEW

<!-- ¶5 -->
###### **OWNER INFORMATION**

<!-- ¶6 -->
The 32-MGD Agua Nueva Water Reclamation Facility is the centerpiece of Pima County's Water Campus, **the largest capital program in the County's history** , valued at $750M. The facility treats wastewater through a full advanced treatment train: **new headworks, influent pumping, combined flocculation and grit removal, dissolved air flotation (DAF) clarification for primary treatment and sludge thickening, five-stage Bardenpho activated sludge secondary treatment with step-feed aeration, tertiary filtration, and chloramine-based disinfection** . The facility produces 25 MGD of Arizona Class A+ reclaimed water for reuse and is partially powered by solar energy. Jacobs holds full DBO responsibility, from concept and design through permitting, construction, and long-term O&M, with an annual operations budget of $4.1M.

<!-- ¶6 -->
###### FACILITY OVERVIEW

<!-- ¶7 -->
###### **OWNER INFORMATION**

<!-- ¶8 -->
The City of San Marcos operates a 9-MGD WWTP serving a growing Texas community along the ecologically sensitive San Marcos River. The facility includes a full treatment train consisting of **influent screening, grit removal, primary clarification, biological nutrient removal, secondary clarification, tertiary sand filtration, UV disinfection, post-aeration, biosolids dewatering and disposal, three odor biotrickling filters, and a Type I reclaimed water program** . Reclaimed water supports cooling towers, irrigation, dust control operations, and the City's animal shelter, conserving hundreds of millions of gallons of potable water annually. The plant also interfaces with the City's collection system of lift stations, which are critical during storm events.

**Process and Cost Optimization:** Over time, chemical usage created both cost and environmental pressure at San Marcos. # 8 Innovative and/or Alternative Recommendations

## Page 178

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### **SECTION 8**

<!-- ¶2 -->
###### **MORE THAN AN OPERATOR: OUR INNOVATIVE APPROACH TO O&M**

<!-- ¶3 -->
OCWUT’s RFP emphasizes innovations supported by clear cost and return-oninvestment (ROI) analysis. While our due diligence has allowed us to identify meaningful opportunities, the level of precision required to fully quantify lifecycle costs and ROI will be refined once we have access to detailed operating data, asset performance, and system conditions during contract execution.

<!-- ¶4 -->
That said, we’re not waiting to bring value. Below are ideas that we’re excited to implement. **Innovation is a key part of Jacobs’ DNA.** Once we're on-site, we'll continue to uncover opportunities that make a real difference for OCWUT. The innovations below are aligned with OCWUT’s priorities and based on proven approaches we have successfully implemented at similar facilities. As we assume operations, we’ll validate each opportunity with site-specific data and work collaboratively with OCWUT to confirm costs and quantify benefits. We also understand each will require OCWUT’s approval before implementation.

<!-- ¶5 -->
While our base program delivers the performance OCWUT expects, we also bring a set of focused innovations that can reduce costs and address known system challenges— unlocking measurable gains in reliability, cost control, and operational efficiency.

<!-- ¶6 -->
###### **Alternative Escalation Index Methodology – Saving OCWUT $620,000 Over the First Contract Term**

<!-- ¶7 -->
As an alternative to the RFP’s CPI-U South escalation, Jacobs proposes an approach that better reflects actual wastewater industry cost drivers. Using an index such as CPI Water/Sewer, or a blended CPI/ECI methodology, more closely aligns annual adjustments with real labor and operating cost trends, reducing the need to carry contingency for mismatches between general CPI and utility-specific escalation.

<!-- ¶8 -->
By aligning escalation with actual cost conditions, we can lower the Base Fee through reduced pricing contingency. This approach provides an estimated savings to OCWUT of approximately $155,000 annually, or $620,000 over the first contract term.

<!-- ¶9 -->
###### **Discounted Engineering Services to Support Innovation and Improvements**

<!-- ¶10 -->
We’re proud of our “more than an operator” DNA and are eager to engage our team to help OCWUT continue elevating the utility. We are offering engineering support at a 2.6 multiplier (discounted from our market rate of 3.0) each year of our partnership for use on Jacobs-managed wastewater and collection system facilities. This provides OCWUT with practical technical support from a team that understands the facilities and can coordinate seamlessly across operations, design, and construction. Lowercost access to our technical specialists can help OCWUT advance priority studies and planning efforts more efficiently while improving continuity, speed of execution, and long-term decision support.

<!-- ¶11 -->
We’ve got some great ideas about how we can work together, and we look forward to discussing this more with you. Some of these potential ideas include the following:

<!-- ¶12 -->
###### _Odor Study Gap Analysis and Updated Odor Control Report_

<!-- ¶13 -->
We’ll take a structured, action-oriented approach to odor management planning. We’ll review prior OCWUT odor studies, validate current findings against field conditions, and identify where data or scope gaps limit decision making. From there, we’ll complete targeted fieldwork and technical analysis to close those gaps and develop an updated, implementation-ready plan with prioritized odor control actions.

<!-- ¶14 -->
Because odor control is a core performance requirement, this effort will focus on practical, cost-effective solutions that improve compliance, safety, asset protection, and community outcomes. Work will be delivered in phases, allowing OCWUT to act quickly on the highest-impact improvements while building toward longer-term systemwide optimization.

<!-- ¶15 -->
###### _Replica Hydraulic Model for System and Facility Optimization_

<!-- ¶16 -->
We can use our hydraulic modeling tools, together with Replica Process modeling already being provided (see cost proposal for details), to evaluate how flow moves through the treatment system under dry and wet weather conditions. This effort can extend beyond the plants to include key collection system interfaces and the

<!-- ¶17 -->
OCWUT-16-26 | O&M OF WASTEWATER FACILITIES AND SOLIDS MANAGEMENT PROGRAM SERVICES  | **166**

## Page 179

<!-- ¶1 -->
Witcher Pump Station, helping OCWUT better understand hydraulic constraints, wet weather risk points, pump station operating strategies, and plant flow management opportunities. We recognize OCWUT’s current initiative with Black & Veatch for the collection system model. Our effort can leverage this work and provide a combined hydraulic and process perspective, supporting the development of more effective operating procedures, setpoints, and sequencing strategies across the system.

<!-- ¶2 -->
This effort will help improve compliance, reliability, and operational performance by establishing distinct operating strategies for dry and wet weather conditions. Potential benefits include improved wet weather response, better pump station sequencing and wet well level management, more reliable flow distribution through the plants, reduced power consumption, and stronger systemwide planning for odor mitigation and hydraulic bottlenecks.

<!-- ¶3 -->
###### _NexGen Phase 2 Implementation Support_

<!-- ¶4 -->
We can help complete the wastewater treatment plant key asset data needed to support NexGen Phase 2 implementation, including installation dates, replacement

<!-- ¶5 -->
costs, impact indices, and expected useful life. This work would help strengthen the quality and completeness of the asset data foundation needed for more effective enterprise asset management.

<!-- ¶6 -->
This support will keep OCWUT on track for full NexGen Phase 2 implementation, support broader enterprise use of the platform, and provide continued access to our asset management professionals. A more complete and decision-ready asset dataset can also improve lifecycle planning, replacement forecasting, capital prioritization, and long-term asset stewardship.

WE OFFER ACCESS TO INNOVATIVE FINANCING APPROACHES

<!-- ¶14 -->
Jacobs has an experienced team that can assist in identifying and securing funding sources for biosolids treatment including thermal drying and other thermal treatment technologies.

<!-- ¶15 -->
Jacobs recently secured a $9.8 million grant from the USDA for Tulsa, Oklahoma, to help offset the cost of building a $45 million biosolids fertilizer manufacturing center. We also assisted the City of Hendersonville, North Carolina, to secure a $14.5 million principal forgiveness grant from the North Carolina Clean Water State Revolving Fund to pay the entire cost of building a new drying facility to eliminate the need for landfilling. In both cases, funding is being used to build facilities to produce marketable biosolids products instead of relying on disposal options such as landfilling. In addition, Jacobs has developed partnership opportunities with technology providers to build and operate thermal drying/gasification facilities that can be funded by utilities or by a capital investment group to minimize capital outlay for our clients. We look forward to discussing these innovative funding options with you to secure biosolids management security for years to come.

## Page 180

<!-- ¶1 -->
###### _Potential Chemical and Energy Savings Through Intelligent O&M_

<!-- ¶2 -->
OCWUT’s system generates a large amount of operating data every day. The value comes from turning that data into timely decisions that help operators protect compliance, reduce costs, and keep critical assets available. We use Intelligent O&M to connect our wastewater operations experience with advanced data analytics, machine learning, and AI-enabled tools that support practical decisions in the field. If interested, we can use Intelligent O&M to evaluate opportunities for power and chemical optimization while maintaining full regulatory compliance and process reliability. Across OCWUT’s facilities, that means focusing on high-impact systems

<!-- ¶3 -->
such as aeration, pumping, disinfection, odor control, sludge handling, polymer dosing, lime stabilization, and chemical feed systems. Our team will review operating history, SCADA information where available, lab results, weather patterns, equipment status, and chemical and energy trends to identify where adjustments can improve efficiency without creating risk. Operators will remain in control of the process; Intelligent O&M will give them better insight, earlier warning signs, and data-supported recommendations.

<!-- ¶4 -->
**We look forward to further discussing these innovations with you.**

<!-- ¶5 -->
We have used Intelligent O&M at<br>wastewater facilities across the U.S.<br>to support chemical dosing, energy<br>efficiency, asset maintenance, and<br>workforce scheduling. At the City of<br>Wilmington, Delaware’s 168-MGD<br>wastewater treatment facility, Jacobs’<br>predictive tools reduced chlorine use by<br>20%, saving the City $200,000 annually<br>while maintaining compliance. At the<br>City of Vancouver, Washington, where<br>we operate two wastewater treatment<br>plants with a combined capacity of<br>44 MGD, Intelligent O&M optimized<br>polymer dosing for solids dewatering,<br>reducing polymer use by 20% and<br>saving $600,000 annually.<br>We will apply the same<br>disciplined, site-specific<br>approach for OCWUT—<br>using operating data, staff<br>expertise, and AI-enabled<br>tools to identify practical<br>savings opportunities without<br>compromising compliance,<br>safety, or reliability.<br><!-- End of picture text -->
